# Neurovisual: research labs and university directories

Source pages reviewed 2026-09-15. 75 detailed profiles; 56 institutions.

## Method

- Read each lab’s official website or institutional profile, then its research, publication or news pages. Use primary papers or official project pages to support specific claims.
- Record exactly what was accessed: page content, a publication record or abstract, or an unavailable page. Keep failed pages and official alternatives in each profile’s review notes.
- Separate the date a source was published from the date it was checked. “Newest dated source found” describes this review’s evidence, not an exhaustive publication search.
- Attach source references to research summaries, selected projects, progress measures and study context. Distinguish completed experiments, preprints, protocols, active studies and future plans.
- Read university discovery pages to extend the directory beyond individual profiles. Record access limits and avoid inferring an active roster or leadership role from an old project page.
- Use project-specific progress measures. Human participants, electrode sites, concurrent recording channels, modeled neurons and clinical-study registrations are different quantities.

This is a selective survey, not a census of every neuroengineering lab. Source dates and review dates are distinct.

## Neurorehabilitation Systems

**Aalborg University · Denmark**

Investigators: Strahinja Dosen

Areas: Rehabilitation & prosthetics; Neural interfaces; Neural decoding

Strahinja Dosen leads Aalborg’s Neurorehabilitation Systems group in the Department of Health Science and Technology. The group studies control and artificial sensory feedback for prostheses, exoskeletons and functional electrical stimulation, with an emphasis on usable assistance for impaired sensory–motor function. [1](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/organisations/neurorehabilitation-systems/) [2](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/persons/sdosen/)

Its interfaces combine myoelectric or brain-derived control with haptic feedback and robotic assistance. The refreshed project register identifies semi-autonomous prostheses and individualized myoelectric control as ongoing programmes; a 2026 review examines why encouraging feedback results do not yet identify a universally best design. [1](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/organisations/neurorehabilitation-systems/) [2](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/persons/sdosen/)

**Methods:** Myoelectric control; Brain-machine interfaces; Haptic feedback; Functional electrical stimulation; Robotic prosthesis control; Human usability assessment.

**Signals:** EMG; Neural control signals; Sensory feedback; Movement and user-reported outcomes.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [Design and impact of systems for artificial sensory feedback in lower-limb prostheses](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/publications/design-and-impact-of-systems-for-artificial-sensory-feedback-in-l/) — 2026-06.

- Added the June 2026 review and separated its literature counts from device or participant counts.
- Rechecked active project dates and supervisory role on the university register.

- [Neurorehabilitation Systems — organisation profile](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/organisations/neurorehabilitation-systems/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Group leadership, department, research aims and current project/output listings.
- [Strahinja Dosen — research profile and projects](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/persons/sdosen/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Current professor/head role, research interests and dated 2025–2029 project records.
- [Romain Valette PhD defense and thesis abstract](https://www.hst.aau.dk/phd-defense-by-romain-valette-e137787) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Thesis abstract: lower-limb feedback, user priorities and uneven biomechanical outcomes.
- [Design and impact of systems for artificial sensory feedback in lower-limb prostheses](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/publications/design-and-impact-of-systems-for-artificial-sensory-feedback-in-l/) — Abstract / publication record read; checked 2026-09-15. Institutional publication record and full abstract read; supports 63-study/81-system synthesis and limits, not full-text reanalysis.

### Projects

#### Semi-autonomous and individualized control

*Registered doctoral and assistive-technology research*

The register lists a 2026–2029 doctoral project on human–machine interfacing for semi-autonomous robotic prostheses, with Dosen as a supervisor. A separate 2025–2027 project studies individualized general-purpose myoelectric control. These are registered research programmes, not completed efficacy results. [2](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/persons/sdosen/)

#### Lower-limb sensory feedback: evidence and usability

*Published evidence synthesis and human-feedback research*

A June 2026 review analyzed 63 studies and 81 feedback systems. It found substantial design heterogeneity and limitations in ecological validity and practical usability, preventing identification of one optimal configuration. The 2025 Valette thesis summary likewise reports user-confidence benefits that were not accompanied by consistent biomechanical gains. [3](https://www.hst.aau.dk/phd-defense-by-romain-valette-e137787) [4](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/publications/design-and-impact-of-systems-for-artificial-sensory-feedback-in-l/)

### Progress to track: Function, confidence and practical usability

The review counts studies and feedback configurations, not participants or a lab device’s electrode capacity. Functional, cognitive and subjective outcomes need separate assessment. [3](https://www.hst.aau.dk/phd-defense-by-romain-valette-e137787) [4](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/publications/design-and-impact-of-systems-for-artificial-sensory-feedback-in-l/)

### Study context

The programme targets rehabilitation, but active project listings are plans. The reviewed feedback literature does not justify a single best system or guaranteed benefit across amputation levels and daily activities. [2](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/persons/sdosen/) [3](https://www.hst.aau.dk/phd-defense-by-romain-valette-e137787) [4](https://vbn.aau.dk/en/publications/design-and-impact-of-systems-for-artificial-sensory-feedback-in-l/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: When does sensory feedback improve independent function beyond what a well-designed semi-autonomous controller already provides?

## BrainGate — Brown Research Program

**Brown University · United States**

Investigators: Leigh Hochberg; John Simeral

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Brown’s BrainGate programme is part of a multisite clinical collaboration developing intracortical interfaces for communication and assistive control. Brown’s current project page lists Leigh Hochberg and John Simeral among its leads. John Donoghue became professor emeritus in July 2026 and continues BrainGate work. [1](https://carney.brown.edu/research-projects/braingate) [4](https://medical.brown.edu/news/2026-06-10/john-donoghue-retirement)

Implanted microelectrode arrays measure movement-related cortical activity. Decoders translate attempted actions into computer commands; engineering and clinical studies separately address communication performance, wireless acquisition and practical home use. [1](https://carney.brown.edu/research-projects/braingate) [4](https://medical.brown.edu/news/2026-06-10/john-donoghue-retirement)

**Methods:** Intracortical microelectrode arrays; Real-time movement decoding; Percutaneous wireless acquisition; Home computer and assistive-device tasks.

**Signals:** Broadband intracortical signals; Movement-related neural features; Cursor trajectories and selections; Task performance over time.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [A Brain Science Legend Retires from Brown](https://medical.brown.edu/news/2026-06-10/john-donoghue-retirement) — 2026-06-10.

- Added the 2026 attempted-finger typing study.
- Updated Donoghue’s status from the official retirement announcement.
- Kept the 2021 wireless result explicitly historical and percutaneous.

- [Brown Carney Institute — BrainGate](https://carney.brown.edu/research-projects/braingate) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Programme, current listed leads and assistive-device research scope.
- [Home Use of a Percutaneous Wireless Intracortical Brain-Computer Interface by Individuals With Tetraplegia](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8218873/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Abstract and introduction read: two-participant wireless comparison, pedestal configuration and 24-hour home recording example.
- [Brain computer interface enables rapid communication for two people with paralysis](https://www.brown.edu/news/2026-03-16/braingate-rapid-communication) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. University account of the two-participant attempted-finger typing study and investigational scope.
- [A Brain Science Legend Retires from Brown](https://medical.brown.edu/news/2026-06-10/john-donoghue-retirement) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Donoghue’s July 1 emeritus transition and continuing BrainGate involvement.

### Projects

#### Attempted-finger typing

*Human clinical research reported in 2026*

Brown’s March 2026 account describes a QWERTY typing neuroprosthesis tested by two participants, one with ALS and one with cervical spinal-cord injury. Attempted finger movements map onto keyboard positions. This is an investigational clinical result, not a generally available communication device. [3](https://www.brown.edu/news/2026-03-16/braingate-rapid-communication)

#### Wireless home BCI: historical milestone

*Human home-use feasibility study, 2021*

The 2021 paper tested wireless broadband transmission in two participants and reports a 24-hour recording example in one participant at home. The transmitters attached to percutaneous pedestals; wireless telemetry did not make this a fully implanted system. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8218873/)

### Progress to track: Communication and operating independence

Typing performance, recording duration, setup burden and assistance requirements are different outcomes. The two studies do not establish universally independent or lifelong use. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8218873/) [3](https://www.brown.edu/news/2026-03-16/braingate-rapid-communication)

### Study context

BrainGate remains a clinical research programme. Its communication demonstrations motivate further development but should not be presented as regulatory approval or routine treatment availability. [1](https://carney.brown.edu/research-projects/braingate) [3](https://www.brown.edu/news/2026-03-16/braingate-rapid-communication)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which parts of daily BCI operation still require assistance, and can those dependencies be removed without sacrificing signal quality or safety?

## N3 Neuroengineering and Nanophotonics Laboratory

**Brown University · United States**

Investigators: Arto Nurmikko

Areas: Neural interfaces; Bioelectronics; Neuroimaging; Neural decoding

Arto Nurmikko’s N3 laboratory develops distributed implantable microsystems and optical measurement methods. Its neurograin work explores wireless networks of small sensing or stimulation nodes; a separate programme uses time- and space-resolved near-infrared imaging. [1](https://nurmikko.engin.brown.edu/research/) [3](https://carney.brown.edu/research-projects/neurograins)

Individual CMOS chiplets combine neural circuitry, wireless power and communication. An external hub coordinates the network. The laboratory’s research page identifies power transfer, telecom, embedded processing and hermetic packaging as engineering priorities; its 2025 publication list adds relay-coil and scattering-media imaging work. [1](https://nurmikko.engin.brown.edu/research/) [3](https://carney.brown.edu/research-projects/neurograins)

**Methods:** Low-power CMOS chiplets; RF wireless power and telemetry; Time-division multiple access; Hermetic microsystem packaging; Functional near-infrared spectroscopy.

**Signals:** Extracellular cortical activity; Evoked stimulation responses; Near-infrared optical signals; Hemodynamic signal carriers.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [Shielded Relay Coil Design to Optimize WPT and SAR for Distributed Wireless Brain Implants](https://nurmikko.engin.brown.edu/publications/) — 2025.

- Added the 2025 relay-coil publication direction without inventing performance results.
- Retained and qualified the 2021 48-versus-770 distinction.

- [N3 Lab research](https://nurmikko.engin.brown.edu/research/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Full research-page body read through direct HTTP after web-reader failure; distributed implants, sensory-cortex aims and near-infrared programme.
- [Neural recording and stimulation using wireless networks of microimplants](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41928-021-00631-8) — Abstract / publication record read; checked 2026-09-15. Publisher abstract and figure captions read; supports 48 acute rat nodes and potential 770-node scaling.
- [Brown Carney Institute — Neurograins](https://carney.brown.edu/research-projects/neurograins) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Nurmikko leadership and architectural functions; broad capacity statements are not treated as in-vivo demonstrations.
- [N3 Lab publications](https://nurmikko.engin.brown.edu/publications/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Actual 2025 publication entries read. No newer dated research entry was located on this list.

### Projects

#### Distributed neurograin recording

*Acute rodent research, 2021*

The 2021 Nature Electronics abstract reports 48 individually addressed neurograins on a rat cortical surface for acute recording. The 770-node figure is a potential scaling result from link calculations and characterization, not an implanted 770-node demonstration. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41928-021-00631-8)

#### Power-transfer and optical-imaging development

*2025 engineering publications; ongoing research directions*

The lab lists a 2025 conference contribution on shielded relay-coil design for wireless power transfer and specific absorption rate, alongside optical tracking through scattering media. These entries establish recent engineering directions; their listing alone does not establish chronic neural-recording performance. [1](https://nurmikko.engin.brown.edu/research/) [4](https://nurmikko.engin.brown.edu/publications/)

### Progress to track: Demonstrated node network versus link capacity

Forty-eight acute recording nodes and a projected 770-node communication configuration are different evidence levels. Chronic packaging and useful neural yield require separate tests. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41928-021-00631-8)

### Study context

The inspected sources describe a research platform and target applications, including sensory-cortex experiments. They do not demonstrate chronic human use or a clinically deployed thousand-channel implant. [1](https://nurmikko.engin.brown.edu/research/) [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41928-021-00631-8) [3](https://carney.brown.edu/research-projects/neurograins)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: As the node network grows, which constraint fails first: delivered power, communication scheduling, chronic encapsulation, or the biological usefulness of additional sites?

2. [Neural recording and stimulation using wireless networks of microimplants](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41928-021-00631-8) — Paper; 2021-08-12. ## Andersen Laboratory

**California Institute of Technology · United States**

Investigators: Richard Andersen

Areas: Neural decoding; Neural interfaces; Neuroimaging; Computational neuroscience; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Richard Andersen’s laboratory studies how posterior parietal and motor populations represent intended and observed actions. It develops human brain–machine interfaces and, with imaging collaborators, functional-ultrasound methods for relating brain activity to behavior. [1](https://www.vis.caltech.edu/research) [3](https://www.vis.caltech.edu/)

The group records single-neuron and field activity during cognitive and motor tasks and combines decoded intentions with robotic control. Functional ultrasound is a separate hemodynamic readout. Recent work also tests how behavioral context changes the encoding of observed actions. [1](https://www.vis.caltech.edu/research) [3](https://www.vis.caltech.edu/)

**Methods:** Posterior parietal intracortical recording; Goal and grasp-intention decoding; Shared robotic control and machine vision; Functional ultrasound neuroimaging; Intracortical sensory stimulation.

**Signals:** Single-neuron activity; Local field potentials; Ultrasound hemodynamic signals; Movement goals and behavior.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [First Findings of Mirror-like Activity in Individual Human Neurons](https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/first-findings-of-mirror-like-activity-in-individual-human-neurons) — 2026-08-24.

- Replaced an exclusively 2021 imaging account with the 2024 human cranial-window study.
- Added August 2026 action-context work and separated preprints from published results.

- [Andersen Lab research programmes](https://www.vis.caltech.edu/research) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Research-page body read: cognitive decoding, shared robotics, sensory stimulation, task context and ultrasound.
- [A Window into the Brain](https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/window-into-the-brain) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Human ultrasound proof of concept, clinical craniectomy context and transparent cranial window.
- [Andersen Lab — current research and publications](https://www.vis.caltech.edu/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Current investigator and dated 2025–2026 publication/preprint list read.
- [First Findings of Mirror-like Activity in Individual Human Neurons](https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/first-findings-of-mirror-like-activity-in-individual-human-neurons) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Two-participant action-observation experiment and contextual interpretation; broad neuron-count language in news is not reused.

### Projects

#### Context-dependent action representation

*Human intracortical physiology, 2026*

An August 2026 Cell study listed by the lab and described by Caltech examined attempted and observed actions in two participants with tetraplegia. The reported mirror-like responses depended on task context; the result is not evidence that all observed actions are automatically represented as intended movement. [3](https://www.vis.caltech.edu/) [4](https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/first-findings-of-mirror-like-activity-in-individual-human-neurons)

#### Human ultrasound through a cranial window

*Human imaging proof of concept, 2024*

The 2024 proof of concept measured task-related brain activity in a person whose clinical cranial reconstruction incorporated an ultrasound-transparent window. This advances beyond the earlier nonhuman-primate work but still requires surgical cranial access; it is not an intact-skull consumer BCI. [2](https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/window-into-the-brain)

### Progress to track: Context, sensing modality and decoded function

Recorded units, ultrasound image coverage and decoded actions are separate scales. Physiological representation and demonstrated assistive-device performance also answer different questions. [1](https://www.vis.caltech.edu/research) [2](https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/window-into-the-brain) [4](https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/first-findings-of-mirror-like-activity-in-individual-human-neurons)

### Study context

Human implants and ultrasound-window studies are investigational. The lab’s news list includes newer preprints, but they are not silently treated as peer-reviewed clinical outcomes. [2](https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/window-into-the-brain) [3](https://www.vis.caltech.edu/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: When should the person specify a goal and let a robot execute it, and when does that delegation remove useful control or agency?

## Biomedical Functional Imaging and Neuroengineering Laboratory

**Carnegie Mellon University · United States**

Investigators: Bin He

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Neuroimaging; Neuromodulation

Bin He’s laboratory develops noninvasive brain interfaces, electrophysiological source imaging and focused-ultrasound neuromodulation. Its current work connects adaptive signal analysis with human training, rather than treating a decoder and its user as independent components. [1](https://www.cmu.edu/bme/helab/) [2](https://www.cmu.edu/bme/helab/People/faculty/bhe.html)

Scalp EEG supports motor-imagery control; anatomical and computational models estimate underlying brain sources. Ultrasound and electrical stimulation form a separate modulation programme. The lab’s latest inspected research concerns tactile guidance and adaptive decoding during BCI acquisition. [1](https://www.cmu.edu/bme/helab/) [2](https://www.cmu.edu/bme/helab/People/faculty/bhe.html)

**Methods:** Scalp EEG motor-imagery decoding with EEGNet; Anatomically constrained EEG/MEG source imaging; Deep-learning model fine-tuning with robotic feedback; Transcranial focused-ultrasound neuromodulation.

**Signals:** Scalp EEG sensorimotor activity; MEG and reconstructed cortical source activity; Ultrasound-evoked electrophysiological responses.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [Joint human-machine learning improves BCI outcomes](https://engineering.cmu.edu/news-events/news/2026/07/16-human-machine-learning.html) — 2026-07-16.

- Added July 2026 joint-learning study, population and task definitions.
- Kept 2025 finger results distinct from 2026 cursor tasks.

- [He Laboratory research and news](https://www.cmu.edu/bme/helab/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Current programme and dated research links read.
- [Bin He faculty research profile](https://www.cmu.edu/bme/helab/People/faculty/bhe.html) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Faculty identity and source-imaging/neuromodulation research scope.
- [EEG-based brain-computer interface enables real-time robotic hand control at individual finger level](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12209421/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Abstract and introduction read: 21 experienced able-bodied users and two-/three-class tasks.
- [Sensory-guided human-machine joint learning accelerates the acquisition of motor imagery brain computer interface control](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-75435-5) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Abstract and introduction read: 31 naïve users, tactile guidance, adaptive sample reweighting and defined accuracy measures.
- [Joint human-machine learning improves BCI outcomes](https://engineering.cmu.edu/news-events/news/2026/07/16-human-machine-learning.html) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Confirms able-bodied population, wrist vibrotactile actuators and investigational interpretation.

### Projects

#### Sensory-guided human–machine joint learning

*Human laboratory study, 2026*

The July 2026 paper tested 31 BCI-naïve participants. Tactile guidance shaped user strategies while sample reweighting adapted the decoder. The study reports mean online discrete accuracies of 86.0% for one-dimensional and 77.5% for two-dimensional motor-imagery tasks. [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-75435-5) [5](https://engineering.cmu.edu/news-events/news/2026/07/16-human-machine-learning.html)

#### Robotic finger control

*Human laboratory study, 2025*

The 2025 study decoded executed or imagined individual finger movements in 21 able-bodied experienced BCI users. Reported motor-imagery accuracies were 80.56% for two-finger and 60.61% for three-finger classification. These are constrained classification tasks, not independent control of every finger. [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12209421/)

### Progress to track: Learning, task accuracy and generalization

The 2025 and 2026 studies used different user experience and task definitions; their percentages should not be treated as a single longitudinal performance curve. Neither accuracy is an electrode count. [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12209421/) [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-75435-5)

### Study context

The new studies establish laboratory feasibility in able-bodied participants. Clinical usefulness, sustained home operation and performance in people with paralysis require separate evaluation. [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12209421/) [5](https://engineering.cmu.edu/news-events/news/2026/07/16-human-machine-learning.html)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can finger-level scalp decoding remain reliable across days and new users while source imaging helps choose informative features?

## Byron Yu Laboratory

**Carnegie Mellon University · United States**

Investigators: Byron Yu

Areas: Computational neuroscience; Neural decoding; Neural interfaces

Byron Yu’s Carnegie Mellon group studies how population activity supports perception, cognition and action. Its research combines statistical modeling with brain–computer interfaces used both as assistive technology and as experimental tools for testing neural computation. [1](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/research.html) [4](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/)

Dimensionality reduction summarizes coordinated activity patterns, while BCI mappings allow researchers to challenge what a population can produce. The laboratory’s 2025–2026 publication list spans dynamical constraints, state-dependent stimulation and causal uses of BCIs. [1](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/research.html) [4](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/)

**Methods:** Factor analysis of neural population activity; Alignment of low-dimensional neural manifolds; Fixed Kalman decoding with adaptive signal stabilization; BCI tasks that challenge neural activity trajectories.

**Signals:** Intracortical population spike counts; Low-dimensional latent neural states; Cursor behavior during closed-loop tasks.

### Projects

#### Dynamical constraints in motor populations

*Nonhuman-primate BCI experiments, 2025*

The 2025 study challenged monkeys to generate neural population trajectories that violated their natural time ordering, including reversal. Animals could not readily produce the required violations, supporting a constraint on the temporal evolution of motor-cortical activity in these tasks. [3](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/papers/ObyDegenhartGrigsbyNN2025.pdf)

#### Maintaining control despite recording changes

*Nonhuman-primate algorithm validation, 2020*

The 2020 manifold-alignment study stabilized input to a fixed BCI decoder during nonhuman-primate cursor control. It tested severe and abrupt recording changes across days. This is a historical algorithmic result, not proof that all chronic human implants work without recalibration. [2](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/papers/DegenhartBishopNatBME2020.pdf)

### Progress to track: Stability and causal constraints

Useful outcomes include control recovered after recording changes and whether imposed neural trajectories can be produced. Latent dimensions are model summaries, not independently measured neurons. [2](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/papers/DegenhartBishopNatBME2020.pdf) [3](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/papers/ObyDegenhartGrigsbyNN2025.pdf)

### Study context

The group links basic population computation with neural-interface design. Its January 2026 listed review on BCIs as causal probes frames scientific opportunities; it is not a new human efficacy trial. [1](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/research.html) [4](https://users.ece.cmu.edu/~byronyu/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which population features remain stable when behavior, motivation, and the recorded neurons all change outside a constrained cursor task?

## Functional Neural Interface Laboratory — Tyler Lab

**Case Western Reserve University · United States**

Investigators: Dustin Tyler

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation; Rehabilitation & prosthetics; Bioelectronics

Dustin Tyler directs the Functional Neural Interface Lab and Human Fusions Institute at Case Western Reserve University. The research combines peripheral-nerve stimulation, muscle-signal recording and human-in-the-loop prosthetic control to restore useful sensation and function. [1](https://case.edu/news/media-relations/dustin-tyler)

Implanted nerve cuffs can evoke percepts referred to a missing hand, while intramuscular signals provide motor commands. The engineering challenge includes making sensing and stimulation operate together wirelessly and measuring functional benefit separately from subjective usefulness. [1](https://case.edu/news/media-relations/dustin-tyler)

**Methods:** Flat-interface nerve electrodes; Peripheral nerve stimulation; Sensorized prosthetic hands; Surface EMG control; Home-use and sensory psychophysics studies.

**Signals:** Contact pressure and hand aperture; Muscle EMG; Evoked tactile percepts; Functional and psychosocial outcomes.

### Projects

#### iSens wireless bidirectional system

*First-in-human technical feasibility, 2025*

The April 2025 case series implanted two people with transradial limb loss using nerve cuffs and intramuscular electrodes. One participant used simultaneous sensory stimulation and myoelectric control of a three-degree-of-freedom prosthetic system. Initial controller tests did not show significant benefit from restored sensation, although the participant found it helpful. [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12020317/)

#### Sensory-enabled home prosthesis use

*Historical human home-use study, 2018*

The 2018 study examined autonomous home/community use with a neural-connected sensory prosthesis and reported functional and psychosocial benefits. Its prolonged-use context differs from the initial controller tests in the later wireless study. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-018-26952-x)

### Progress to track: Bidirectional stability and meaningful benefit

The 2025 series reports more than two years of stability in one participant and four months in the second, whose system was removed after infection of unknown origin. Duration, complications and task performance must be reported together. [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12020317/)

### Study context

These are investigational implants and small studies. The iSens paper supports technical feasibility before long-term community trials; it does not establish universal functional improvement or commercial availability. [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12020317/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: What combination of natural sensation, reliable control, and manageable hardware actually increases long-term prosthesis use outside supervised studies?

## Bioelectronic Systems Lab (BIOEE)

**Columbia University · United States**

Investigators: Ken Shepard

Areas: Neural interfaces; Bioelectronics; Neuroimaging

Kenneth Shepard’s Bioelectronic Systems Lab at Columbia integrates circuits and biological interfaces. Its cortical programme centers on BISC, an ultrathin wireless surface array; the wider research portfolio includes optical interfaces, wearable imaging and miniature implantable sensors. [1](https://bioee.ee.columbia.edu/research/)

BISC places electrodes, signal processing, power reception and telemetry on one flexible CMOS substrate. This reduces the separation between the tissue interface and its electronics, while forcing explicit tradeoffs between electrode coverage, simultaneous channels, sampling rate and wireless bandwidth. [1](https://bioee.ee.columbia.edu/research/)

**Methods:** CMOS integrated circuits; Wireless micro-ECoG; Single-photon avalanche detectors; MicroLED integration; Time-domain optical imaging.

**Signals:** Surface cortical field potentials; Fluorescence; Time-resolved near-infrared light.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

- Rechecked the full 2025 paper and preserved species, durations, site/channel distinction and sampling rates.
- Replaced the stale publications table (ending at 2023) with the dated publisher record.

- [A wireless subdural-contained brain–computer interface with 65,536 electrodes and 1,024 channels](https://bioee.ee.columbia.edu/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/s41928-025-01509-9.pdf) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Author-hosted full PDF abstract and ASIC design section read; duration, species, selectable channels and sampling rates.
- [BISC publisher record](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41928-025-01509-9) — Abstract / publication record read; checked 2026-09-15. Publisher abstract and publication metadata read; date checked because the author PDF contains an unfilled online-date placeholder.

### Projects

#### BISC cortical surface interface

*Preclinical wireless cortical recording, 2025*

The December 2025 paper describes 65,536 recording electrodes with a selectable subset of up to 1,024 recorded simultaneously. It reports recordings up to two weeks in pigs and two months in behaving nonhuman primates. The platform is subdural and does not use penetrating electrode shanks. [2](https://bioee.ee.columbia.edu/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/s41928-025-01509-9.pdf)

#### Integrated optical and wearable interfaces

*Research and device-development programmes*

The current research page describes optical BCI tools, near-infrared imaging and wearable ultrasound electronics as separate programmes. These rely on different biological signals and validation pathways; proposed optical advantages should not be read as demonstrated human clinical benefit. [1](https://bioee.ee.columbia.edu/research/)

### Progress to track: Electrode sites, simultaneous channels and bandwidth

The 65,536 physical recording sites are not 65,536 simultaneous channels. The paper supports selectable 256-channel/33.9-kS/s and 1,024-channel/8.475-kS/s configurations; these preserve a sampling-rate versus channel-count tradeoff. [2](https://bioee.ee.columbia.edu/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/s41928-025-01509-9.pdf)

### Study context

The cited BISC study is preclinical. The lab’s human-translation framing describes a programme objective, not a demonstrated approved implant or chronic human BCI outcome. [1](https://bioee.ee.columbia.edu/research/) [2](https://bioee.ee.columbia.edu/wp-content/uploads/2025/12/s41928-025-01509-9.pdf)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Would lower-bandwidth field-potential readout produce more useful simultaneous coverage than preserving spike-band capability at every site?

## Neural Prostheses Research Laboratory — Grill Lab

**Duke University · United States**

Investigators: Warren Grill

Areas: Neuromodulation; Computational neuroscience; Neural interfaces; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Warren Grill’s Duke laboratory investigates how electrical stimulation affects the nervous system and how those mechanisms can improve neural prostheses. It combines computational models, animal experiments and human physiology across brain, spinal and peripheral-nerve stimulation. [1](https://www.neuro.duke.edu/research/faculty-labs/grill-lab) [5](https://grill-lab.pratt.duke.edu/publications)

Electric-field and axon models guide waveform and electrode choices; experiments test recruitment, side effects and functional responses. Recent work includes tools for reproducible nerve-fiber simulation and attempts to avoid unwanted motor activation during vagus-nerve stimulation. [1](https://www.neuro.duke.edu/research/faculty-labs/grill-lab) [5](https://grill-lab.pratt.duke.edu/publications)

**Methods:** Electric-field and axon modeling; Waveform optimization; Animal stimulation and recording; Human clinical physiology.

**Signals:** Neural responses; Evoked muscle activity; Cardiac responses; Movement symptoms.

Abstract and introduction read; swine selectivity experiment. Publisher lists version of record 2026-01-28.
- [PyFibers: an open-source NEURON-Python package](https://journals.plos.org/ploscompbiol/article?id=10.1371/journal.pcbi.1013764) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Abstract, author summary and date read; 11 models and implementation validation.
- [Grill Laboratory — publications](https://grill-lab.pratt.duke.edu/publications) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. 2025–2026 entries read. September 2026 papers used as bibliographic evidence only.

### Projects

#### Selective vagus-nerve block, 2025/2026

*Preclinical swine study*

A swine study paired conventional vagus stimulation with kilohertz-frequency block. Properly adjusted high-frequency amplitude suppressed off-target motor responses while preserving activation of smaller fibers. This establishes a large-animal mechanism, not proven clinical benefit. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-67823-0)

#### PyFibers, 2025

*Computational research tool*

The open-source NEURON-Python package brings 11 published nerve-fiber models into a common framework and validates their implementations against original results. It supports studies of stimulation, block and recording, without substituting simulation for patient testing. [4](https://journals.plos.org/ploscompbiol/article?id=10.1371/journal.pcbi.1013764)

#### Temporal-pattern DBS, 2017

*Animal and human research*

Model-based optimization produced stimulation patterns tested in parkinsonian rats and people with Parkinson’s disease. Comparable symptom relief with reduced pulse requirements is a dated experimental result, not a general claim that any lower-frequency DBS is equivalent. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5516784/)

### Progress to track: Selectivity, efficiency and reproducibility

Relevant measures include unwanted muscle recruitment, activation of intended fibers, stimulation energy and reproducibility of model responses. PyFibers contains 11 models; that count is not an electrode count. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5516784/) [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-67823-0) [4](https://journals.plos.org/ploscompbiol/article?id=10.1371/journal.pcbi.1013764)

### Study context

The laboratory explicitly spans preclinical development and clinical feasibility work. Its 2026 bibliography includes spinal-cord, sacral, vagus and cortical stimulation studies; individual methods retain their own evidence stage. [1](https://www.neuro.duke.edu/research/faculty-labs/grill-lab) [5](https://grill-lab.pratt.duke.edu/publications)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can a model predict an individual’s therapeutic and off-target responses well enough to reduce trial-and-error programming?

## Viventi Laboratory

**Duke University · United States**

Investigators: Jonathan Viventi

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Bioelectronics

Jonathan Viventi’s Duke group develops flexible cortical-surface electronics and uses their spatially dense recordings to study speech and epilepsy. Recent publications extend hardware development into speech planning and transfer of speech representations between participants. [1](https://viventi.pratt.duke.edu/node) [4](https://viventi.pratt.duke.edu/publications)

Thin-film micro-electrocorticography records local field activity at closely spaced cortical sites. The group combines array design, signal processing and intraoperative recordings; a dense surface array does not imply recording one neuron per contact. [1](https://viventi.pratt.duke.edu/node) [4](https://viventi.pratt.duke.edu/publications)

**Methods:** Flexible thin-film μECoG; Intraoperative cortical recording; Speech decoding; Spatial signal analysis.

**Signals:** Cortical surface potentials; Speech-task neural activity.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [Distinct neural processes link speech planning and execution](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41562-026-02546-w) — 2026-08-13.

- Added August 2026 speech-mechanism paper after reading publisher abstract.
- Removed undated legacy claims that all existing arrays are passive.
- Kept surface recordings distinct from single-neuron counts.

- [Viventi Laboratory — research overview](https://viventi.pratt.duke.edu/node) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Content read directly; supports the cited scope.
- [High-resolution neural recordings improve the accuracy of speech decoding](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-023-42555-1) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Abstract and introduction read; intraoperative μECoG speech experiment and comparison scope.
- [Distinct neural processes link speech planning and execution](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41562-026-02546-w) — Abstract / publication record read; checked 2026-09-15. Publisher abstract and publication metadata read; planning/execution results only.
- [Viventi Laboratory — publications](https://viventi.pratt.duke.edu/publications) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. 2024–2026 entries read; confirms recent speech, epilepsy and device work.

### Projects

#### Speech planning and execution, 2026

*Human research*

Intracranial recordings at multiple spatial scales distinguished discrete speech-unit representations during planning from rapidly changing sequences during execution. The accessible paper abstract supports a human neurophysiology result, not an independently usable speech prosthesis. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41562-026-02546-w)

#### High-resolution speech recordings, 2023

*Human intraoperative research*

The intraoperative study compared μECoG with conventional intracranial recordings and reported improved speech decoding with finer sampling. This is evidence for spatial resolution in that experiment; it does not establish chronic home-use reliability. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-023-42555-1)

### Progress to track: Spatial sampling and decoding quality

Compare electrode spacing, signal quality, coverage and decoding under the same task; the studies do not provide a universal electrode-count growth rate. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-023-42555-1) [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41562-026-02546-w)

### Study context

The lab emphasizes stable, usable devices and sharing technology with researchers. The retained evidence concerns experimental recording systems and human intraoperative studies; it is not a claim of an approved communication product. [1](https://viventi.pratt.duke.edu/node) [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-023-42555-1)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: At a fixed connector and power budget, does closer spacing or coverage of another cortical region add more useful information for the intended task?

## Courtine Laboratory / .NeuroRestore

**EPFL · Switzerland**

Investigators: Grégoire Courtine; Jocelyne Bloch

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

The Courtine laboratory and the collaborative .NeuroRestore program led by Grégoire Courtine and Jocelyne Bloch study how stimulation and rehabilitation can restore movement after nervous-system injury. The work connects animal circuit experiments with individually configured human neuroprostheses. [1](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/courtine-lab/) [3](https://actu.epfl.ch/news/robotics-and-spinal-stimulation-restore-movement-3)

The laboratory combines spinal-circuit analysis, epidural stimulation, movement sensing and rehabilitation robotics. A brain–spine interface adds cortical recording and a decoder to adjust spinal stimulation; other projects use movement sensors without a cortical implant. [1](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/courtine-lab/) [3](https://actu.epfl.ch/news/robotics-and-spinal-stimulation-restore-movement-3)

**Methods:** Epidural electrical stimulation; Cortical decoding; Rehabilitation robotics; Movement sensing; Animal circuit experiments.

**Signals:** Cortical field potentials; Limb kinematics; Muscle activity.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [Robotics and spinal stimulation restore movement in paralysis](https://actu.epfl.ch/news/robotics-and-spinal-stimulation-restore-movement-3) — 2025-03-13.

- Added 2025 robotics integration with sample size and limitations.
- Separated cortical-decoder BSI from sensor-controlled spinal stimulation.

- [Walking naturally after spinal cord injury using a brain–spine interface](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-023-06094-5) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Publisher abstract and main text read; one participant, implanted cortical/spinal systems and year-long stability.
- [Robotics and spinal stimulation restore movement in paralysis](https://actu.epfl.ch/news/robotics-and-spinal-stimulation-restore-movement-3) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Full institutional research report read; five-person proof of concept and explicit need for longer-term trials.
- [Courtine Laboratory — location](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/courtine-lab/location/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Geneva Campus Biotech address read; clinical collaboration also involves Lausanne.

### Projects

#### Robotics plus spinal stimulation, 2025

*Human proof of concept*

A five-person proof-of-concept study combined epidural spinal stimulation with rehabilitation devices such as treadmills, exoskeletons and cycling systems. Motion sensors synchronized stimulation with movement. The institutional report describes muscle engagement and some stimulation-off improvements, while stating that longer-term trials remain necessary. [3](https://actu.epfl.ch/news/robotics-and-spinal-stimulation-restore-movement-3)

#### Brain–spine interface, 2023

*Human single-participant study*

A participant with chronic tetraplegia used implanted cortical recording and spinal stimulation to control standing and walking. The paper reports stability over a year and use at home. This single-participant result cannot be generalized to all injuries. [2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-023-06094-5)

### Progress to track: Participants, movement function and durability

Keep the 2025 five-person robotics study distinct from the 2023 single-participant brain–spine interface. Functional measures and duration are more informative here than a pooled electrode count. [2](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-023-06094-5) [3](https://actu.epfl.ch/news/robotics-and-spinal-stimulation-restore-movement-3)

### Study context

The program spans mechanistic animal work and human studies with clinical and engineering collaborators. The 2025 robotics integration remains a proof of concept, with long-term benefit requiring further clinical evaluation. [1](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/courtine-lab/) [3](https://actu.epfl.ch/news/robotics-and-spinal-stimulation-restore-movement-3)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which patient and circuit characteristics predict durable gains from combining intention decoding with stimulation and intensive rehabilitation?

## Hummel Laboratory

**EPFL · Switzerland**

Investigators: Friedhelm Hummel

Areas: Neuromodulation; Neuroimaging; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Friedhelm Hummel’s EPFL laboratory studies human plasticity and recovery using behavioral experiments, brain imaging and noninvasive stimulation. Its work includes motor learning in healthy participants and development of interventions for stroke and other neurological conditions. [1](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/hummel-lab/research/) [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41582-025-01137-z)

The group combines EEG, structural and functional MRI, TMS and electrical stimulation. Temporal interference uses interacting high-frequency electric fields to investigate deeper targets; target engagement and clinical benefit must be evaluated separately. [1](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/hummel-lab/research/) [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41582-025-01137-z)

**Methods:** TMS; Transcranial electrical stimulation; Temporal interference stimulation; EEG; MRI; Behavioral assessment.

**Signals:** EEG; BOLD fMRI; Motor-task performance.

### Projects

#### Striatal temporal interference, 2023

*Human experimental study*

Computational modeling, fMRI and behavioral experiments supported modulation of striatal activity and motor learning in humans, including older healthy participants. The study provides experimental target-engagement evidence, not proof of stroke rehabilitation efficacy. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-023-01457-7)

#### NIMBUS stroke study — announced

*Planned clinical study; timing not confirmed*

The current lab page announces a multicenter study combining temporal-interference stimulation with physiotherapy for upper-limb recovery after stroke. It says design, eligibility and recruitment details will be supplied later; the page does not establish that enrollment has begun. [3](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/hummel-lab/research/current-research-projects/nimbus/)

#### Clinical evidence review, 2025

*Review, not a new treatment trial*

A coauthored review highlights small samples, population heterogeneity and variable stimulation protocols as obstacles to firm conclusions, and discusses individualized, biomarker-guided stimulation. [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41582-025-01137-z)

### Progress to track: Target engagement and patient outcomes

Evaluate physiological modulation, task performance and functional recovery as separate outcomes. The announced multicenter trial page provides no participant total or efficacy result. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-023-01457-7) [3](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/hummel-lab/research/current-research-projects/nimbus/) [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41582-025-01137-z)

### Study context

The laboratory links clinical partners in Geneva, Sion and Lucerne with stimulation development. Its planned NIMBUS work should remain labeled as an announced study until dated trial or recruitment information supports a stronger status. [1](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/hummel-lab/research/) [3](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/hummel-lab/research/current-research-projects/nimbus/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can individualized stimulation parameters reliably improve recovery beyond training alone while maintaining accurate targeting during home use?

## Laboratory for Soft Bioelectronic Interfaces

**EPFL · Switzerland**

Investigators: Stéphanie Lacour

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation

Stéphanie Lacour’s Laboratory for Soft Bioelectronic Interfaces develops electronics whose mechanics better match tissue. Work spans implantable neural interfaces, skin electronics and platforms for cell electrophysiology. Lacour remains laboratory head and became EPFL Vice President for Support to Strategic Initiatives in January 2025. [1](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/lsbi/lsbi-2/research/) [4](https://neuro-x.epfl.ch/en/news/were-about-to-see-a-paradigm-shift-in-the-manufacture-of-implants/)

The team adapts thin-film microfabrication, elastomers and compliant conductors to make electrical interfaces that bend or stretch. Mechanical compatibility is a design strategy; long-term stability and clinical benefit still require direct evidence. [1](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/lsbi/lsbi-2/research/) [4](https://neuro-x.epfl.ch/en/news/were-about-to-see-a-paradigm-shift-in-the-manufacture-of-implants/)

**Methods:** Soft thin-film electronics; Elastomer-compatible microfabrication; Electrical stimulation; Electrophysiological recording.

**Signals:** Evoked cortical potentials; Behavioral auditory discrimination; Electrical tissue signals.

Institutional interview read; laboratory leadership, January 2025 role change and stated stability/manufacturing limits.

### Projects

#### Soft auditory brainstem implant, 2025

*Nonhuman-primate study*

A dual-site brainstem/cortex system in macaques tested electrically evoked hearing with a soft multichannel implant. Cortical responses differed by stimulation site, and behavior over several months distinguished adjacent channels. These are nonhuman-primate results, not evidence of restored human speech intelligibility. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-025-01378-9)

#### Soft depth probes — 2026 publication

*Published preclinical work; details not reviewed*

The current laboratory bibliography lists “Soft Depth Neural Probes Enable Chronic Recordings from the Rat Brainstem.” The title identifies rat-brainstem recording as a new direction; detailed duration or performance claims are withheld because the paper itself was not accessible in this review. [3](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/lsbi/lsbi-2/publications/)

### Progress to track: Mechanical conformity, selectivity and stability

For the auditory implant, distinguish cortical response selectivity and behavioral channel discrimination from human hearing outcomes. No single array-size number captures these different interfaces. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-025-01378-9) [4](https://neuro-x.epfl.ch/en/news/were-about-to-see-a-paradigm-shift-in-the-manufacture-of-implants/)

### Study context

In the institutional interview, Lacour describes soft interfaces as preclinical or at an early stage of human testing and explicitly notes limited long-term stability data. She is a Neuro X cofounder and former director, not its current director. [4](https://neuro-x.epfl.ch/en/news/were-about-to-see-a-paradigm-shift-in-the-manufacture-of-implants/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which combinations of soft materials, barrier layers and interconnect geometry preserve recording quality during years of tissue motion?

## Bio Engineering Laboratory

**ETH Zurich · Switzerland**

Investigators: Andreas Hierlemann

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neural interfaces; Computational neuroscience

Andreas Hierlemann’s Bio Engineering Laboratory at ETH Zurich’s Basel campus builds microsystems for measuring and manipulating cells and tissues. Its neural work uses high-density microelectrode arrays with cultures, brain slices, organoids and retinal tissue rather than treating all platforms as implanted BCIs. [1](https://bsse.ethz.ch/department/building-bss/tour/e-floor/research-at-dbsse---forschung-am-dbsse.html) [2](https://bsse.ethz.ch/news-and-events/d-bsse-news/2026/05/rewiring-neural-connections.html)

CMOS arrays provide spatially distributed stimulation and electrophysiological readout. Experiments connect device precision to biological questions such as activity-dependent changes in connectivity and the coordination of retinal output. [1](https://bsse.ethz.ch/department/building-bss/tour/e-floor/research-at-dbsse---forschung-am-dbsse.html) [2](https://bsse.ethz.ch/news-and-events/d-bsse-news/2026/05/rewiring-neural-connections.html)

**Methods:** CMOS high-density microelectrode arrays; Timed electrical stimulation; Patch clamp; Cell and tissue electrophysiology.

**Signals:** Extracellular spikes; Evoked responses; Retinal population activity.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

- [Rewiring neural connections](https://bsse.ethz.ch/news-and-events/d-bsse-news/2026/05/rewiring-neural-connections.html) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Research report read; tissue models, tested-pair denominator, persistence and patch-clamp verification.
- [Meet Annalisa Bucci — studying visual signaling in human retina](https://bsse.ethz.ch/news-and-events/d-bsse-news/2026/01/meet-annalisa-bucci-studying-visual-signaling-in-human-retina.html) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Interview read; human explant recordings and planned axon-guidance work.

### Projects

#### Electrical modification of connections, 2026

*In-vitro and ex-vivo research*

A May institutional report describes precisely timed stimulation of rat neuronal cultures, mouse slices and cerebral organoids on high-density arrays. Connection strength changed in roughly 40% of tested neuronal pairs; some effects lasted at least 90 minutes, with patch-clamp confirmation. This is an in-vitro result, not demonstrated repair in patients. [2](https://bsse.ethz.ch/news-and-events/d-bsse-news/2026/05/rewiring-neural-connections.html)

#### Human retinal signaling, 2026

*Ex-vivo human tissue; platform development*

A January laboratory interview describes high-density-array measurements from human retinal explants and research on coordinated ganglion-cell signals. Proposed microstructures for guiding axons are a research direction, not a demonstrated retinal replacement. [3](https://bsse.ethz.ch/news-and-events/d-bsse-news/2026/01/meet-annalisa-bucci-studying-visual-signaling-in-human-retina.html)

### Progress to track: Perturbable connections and recording coverage

The 40% figure describes tested cell pairs in the reported connectivity experiment, not a fraction of a brain rewired. Recording coverage, tissue model and persistence should accompany any scale claim. [2](https://bsse.ethz.ch/news-and-events/d-bsse-news/2026/05/rewiring-neural-connections.html)

### Study context

ETH identifies MaxWell Biosystems, founded in 2016, as originating from the Hierlemann lab. Its electrophysiology tools support cell assays and preclinical research; this commercial path is distinct from an implanted therapeutic device. [4](https://bsse.ethz.ch/department/spin-offs.html)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: How can dense-array analysis distinguish altered synaptic connectivity from changes in excitability, cell health and recording geometry?

## Müller-Putz Team, Institute of Neural Engineering

**Graz University of Technology · Austria**

Investigators: Gernot Müller-Putz

Areas: Neural decoding; Neural interfaces; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Gernot Müller-Putz leads the Institute of Neural Engineering and the Graz BCI laboratory. The team studies movement decoding, error processing and assistive control, with emphasis on people who cannot use conventional movement-based interfaces. [1](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/research/team-mueller-putz) [3](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/people/gernot-mueller-putz)

EEG processing connects attempted movement to commands for neuroprostheses. The team combines artifact handling, machine learning and end-user experiments; its communication research also includes a separate implantable-system project. [1](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/research/team-mueller-putz) [3](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/people/gernot-mueller-putz)

**Methods:** EEG acquisition; Artifact handling; Movement-intention decoding; Machine learning; Neuroprosthesis control.

**Signals:** EEG; Movement-related cortical potentials; Control errors.

### Projects

#### NeuroGrasp

*Technology development and planned user evaluation*

The current project page describes decoding attempted grasps from EEG to command a neuroprosthesis. It acknowledges that earlier real-time control had excessive errors and proposes machine/deep learning to improve reliability, with future tests in people with spinal-cord injury or amputations. [2](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/research/team-mueller-putz/neurograsp/)

#### INTRECOM

*Collaborative implantable-BCI development*

The team page describes development of a fully implantable BCI for speech decoding and home communication in locked-in syndrome. The stated capabilities are project aims; the page alone does not show a completed home-use trial. [1](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/research/team-mueller-putz)

### Progress to track: Reliable intentional commands

Useful measures include false activations, grasp-selection accuracy and performance during real-life use. The read project pages provide no comparable longitudinal electrode or participant series. [1](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/research/team-mueller-putz) [2](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/research/team-mueller-putz/neurograsp/)

### Study context

Clinical neuroprosthesis research links the Graz group with rehabilitation partners. MoreGrasp (2015–2018) and Feel Your Reach (2016–2021) are historical programs; newer pages identify NeuroGrasp and INTRECOM, but give no dated efficacy results. [1](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/research/team-mueller-putz) [2](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/research/team-mueller-putz/neurograsp/) [4](https://www.tugraz.at/institute/ine/home/)

## Computational Cognitive Engineering Lab

**Hong Kong University of Science and Technology · China**

Investigators: Yiwen Wang

Areas: Neural decoding; Computational neuroscience; Neural interfaces

Yiwen Wang’s Computational Cognitive Engineering Lab at HKUST studies neural plasticity, decoding and adaptive brain–machine interfaces. Its recent spike-generation work is a computational bridge between recorded upstream activity and desired behavior, not a demonstrated biological restoration of a damaged pathway. [1](https://bmi.hkust.edu.hk/) [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s43588-025-00915-5)

The group develops models of how neural patterns adapt and how activity can guide prosthetic actions. The 2026 work uses reinforcement learning to optimize a point-process model against behavioral rewards when downstream neural recordings are unavailable. [1](https://bmi.hkust.edu.hk/) [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s43588-025-00915-5)

**Methods:** Reinforcement learning; Point-process spike generation; Neural decoding; Adaptive BMI modeling.

**Signals:** Recorded upstream neural activity; Generated spike trains; Behavioral rewards.

### Projects

#### Behaviorally reinforced spike generation, 2026

*Computational modeling and evaluation*

The published framework transforms upstream activity into generated spike patterns while maximizing behavior-level reward. The abstract reports movement-modulated patterns and adaptation across decoder settings. Generated spikes must not be counted as additional recorded neurons. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s43588-025-00915-5)

#### Adaptive neural prostheses

*Research program*

The lab describes models of learning and cross-area information flow intended to support prosthetic control that generalizes across movement tasks. Restoring long-term full motor function is an aspiration on the page, not an established patient outcome. [1](https://bmi.hkust.edu.hk/)

### Progress to track: Adaptation and behavior-related model output

Evaluate generated spike statistics, behavioral prediction and transfer between decoder settings. The source does not establish implanted stimulation efficacy or a clinically restored connection. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s43588-025-00915-5)

### Study context

The proposed use is biomimetic stimulation and repair of interregional communication. The available paper abstract establishes a computational encoding framework; implementation in an injured biological pathway remains a separate validation step. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s43588-025-00915-5)

## Neuromechanics and Rehabilitation Technology

**Imperial College London · United Kingdom**

Investigators: Dario Farina

Areas: Neural decoding; Rehabilitation & prosthetics; Neural interfaces

Dario Farina’s Imperial group studies how motor-neuron commands can be inferred from muscles and used in rehabilitation interfaces. Its work links motor-unit physiology, electrode hardware and signal processing to prostheses, exoskeletons and tremor-control research. [1](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neuromechanics-rehabilitation-technology/research/) [4](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neurotechnology/training/mres-neurotechnology/projects/)

High-density surface or intramuscular EMG is decomposed into motor-unit discharge trains. This estimates spinal motor-neuron output through muscle activity; it is not direct recording from implanted spinal-cord electrodes. [1](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neuromechanics-rehabilitation-technology/research/) [4](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neurotechnology/training/mres-neurotechnology/projects/)

**Methods:** High-density surface EMG; Intramuscular EMG; Motor-unit decomposition; Real-time phase tracking; Prosthetic-control modeling.

**Signals:** EMG; Estimated motor-unit discharge trains; Tremor phase.

### Projects

#### Coordination after targeted muscle reinnervation, 2026 preprint

*Human research preprint*

A February-revised preprint studied three participants using high-density intramuscular arrays in reinnervated muscles. It reports preserved agonist–antagonist coordination despite changed anatomy. This small human physiology study informs control strategies without demonstrating a finished prosthetic system. [3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16689)

#### Real-time tremor phase estimation, 2022

*Human method study and single-person demonstration*

EMG from 13 people with essential tremor was decomposed to estimate neural drive and track phase relationships between antagonist muscles. A closed-loop stimulation demonstration involved one participant; it does not establish efficacy in a large treatment trial. [2](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neuromechanics-rehabilitation-technology/publications/?id=1249681&noscript=noscript&respub-t4-action=citation.html)

#### Wearable interfaces — 2026–2027 project directions

*Proposed research projects*

The current training page proposes physiological characterization, ultrasound-based simulation and EMG typing prediction. These descriptions identify planned research rather than completed results. [4](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neurotechnology/training/mres-neurotechnology/projects/)

### Progress to track: Motor units and useful control

Measure decomposition accuracy, command reliability and functional task performance separately. Counts refer to muscle-derived motor units, not cortical electrodes. [1](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neuromechanics-rehabilitation-technology/research/) [2](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neuromechanics-rehabilitation-technology/publications/?id=1249681&noscript=noscript&respub-t4-action=citation.html) [3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16689)

### Study context

The group combines engineering with surgical and rehabilitation collaborations. The Imperial–Meta center page gives a June 2022–June 2026 project duration; continuation beyond that date was not established here. [1](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neuromechanics-rehabilitation-technology/research/) [5](https://www.imperial.ac.uk/neuromechanics-rehabilitation-technology/research/active-projects/imperial-meta/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: How well do decomposed motor-unit commands remain usable after electrode repositioning, fatigue and changes in daily activity?

## NeuRonICS Lab

**Indian Institute of Science · India**

Investigators: Chetan Singh Thakur

Areas: Bioelectronics; Computational neuroscience

Chetan Singh Thakur’s NeuRonICS program at IISc develops brain-inspired signal processing and low-power electronic systems. Current institutional material confirms neuromorphic computing, FPGA and mixed-signal VLSI expertise. The lab’s own pages could not be retrieved, so current biomedical-project claims are limited. [1](https://eecs.iisc.ac.in/people/chetan-singh-thakur/) [3](https://spectrum.ee.iisc.ac.in/articles/neuromorphic-unlimited-sampling/)

The accessible evidence concerns event-based sensing, neural computation and hardware emulation. An electronic retina model or efficient signal-acquisition circuit should not be described as an implanted clinical neural interface. [1](https://eecs.iisc.ac.in/people/chetan-singh-thakur/) [3](https://spectrum.ee.iisc.ac.in/articles/neuromorphic-unlimited-sampling/)

**Methods:** FPGA hardware emulation; Mixed-signal VLSI; Event-based encoding; Signal reconstruction.

**Signals:** Image pixels; Asynchronous sensor events; Electronic test signals.

### Projects

#### FPGA retinal emulator, 2025 preprint

*Hardware/computational preprint*

The authors describe a reconfigurable model with luminance/contrast adaptation and center–surround receptive fields, supporting 128 × 128 images at 200 frames per second. These are digital-emulator specifications, not living-retina recordings or vision restoration. [2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.08943)

#### Neuromorphic unlimited sampling

*Hardware research demonstration*

An IISc collaborator’s research page describes an event-based encoder combined with a conventional ADC for high-dynamic-range acquisition and real-time signal reconstruction. It explicitly credits NeuRonICS hardware collaborators and reports an ICASSP 2024 demonstration. [3](https://spectrum.ee.iisc.ac.in/articles/neuromorphic-unlimited-sampling/)

### Progress to track: Processing capacity and acquisition efficiency

The retinal emulator’s image dimensions and frame rate describe computation. They must not be converted into electrode counts, recorded neurons or clinical capability. [2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.08943)

### Study context

The retained work provides tools for neuromorphic processing and future sensing interfaces. No clinical implant outcome or current high-channel-count human BCI was established from the sources that could be read. [1](https://eecs.iisc.ac.in/people/chetan-singh-thakur/) [2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.08943) [3](https://spectrum.ee.iisc.ac.in/articles/neuromorphic-unlimited-sampling/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: How can event-based computation retain useful biological signal structure while reducing hardware energy and latency?

## BioNICS Lab

**Indian Institute of Technology Bombay · India**

Investigators: Laxmeesha Somappa

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Neuromodulation

Laxmeesha Somappa’s BioNICS group at IIT Bombay designs integrated circuits and systems for neural sensing, classification and stimulation. The work focuses on the electronics needed to process signals under power, area and telemetry constraints. [1](https://www.ee.iitb.ac.in/~laxmeesha/) [3](https://www.ee.iitb.ac.in/~laxmeesha/team.html)

The official research description combines low-noise front ends, on-chip machine learning, compression, programmable stimulators and data-security circuits. These are enabling technologies for neural systems, with evidence stages determined by each design or experiment. [1](https://www.ee.iitb.ac.in/~laxmeesha/) [3](https://www.ee.iitb.ac.in/~laxmeesha/team.html)

**Methods:** Analog front-end design; On-chip classification; Time-division multiplexing; Neural data compression; Programmable stimulation circuits.

**Signals:** Electrophysiological input signals; Classifier features; Electrical stimulation waveforms.

## Vestibular NeuroEngineering Laboratory

**Johns Hopkins University · United States**

Investigators: Charles C. Della Santina

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Charles Della Santina’s Johns Hopkins Vestibular NeuroEngineering Laboratory develops implants that encode head movement as electrical stimulation of vestibular-nerve branches. The goal is to improve balance and stable vision in people with bilateral vestibular loss. [1](https://sites.google.com/site/vestibularneuroengineering/) [5](https://profiles.hopkinsmedicine.org/provider/charles-c-della-santina/2705660?page=1)

Motion sensors drive an implanted inner-ear stimulator. The program combines nerve-interface engineering, vestibular physiology, eye-movement measurements and investigational human studies; this is sensory replacement rather than cortical intention decoding. [1](https://sites.google.com/site/vestibularneuroengineering/) [5](https://profiles.hopkinsmedicine.org/provider/charles-c-della-santina/2705660?page=1)

**Methods:** Vestibular nerve stimulation; Head-motion sensing; Eye-movement measurements; Implant design; Human cohort assessment.

**Signals:** Head rotation; Vestibulo-ocular responses; Patient-reported symptoms.

### Projects

#### Patient-reported outcomes, 2024

*Human early-feasibility cohort*

A nonrandomized cohort study compared 10 implant recipients with 10 applicants awaiting intervention. At six months, recipients reported improved dizziness, disability and one quality-of-life measure, but not the HUI3 measure. Four recipients developed severe/profound hearing loss in the implanted ear and one a correctable moderate-to-severe loss. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10835607/)

#### Electrode placement, 2026

*Nonhuman-primate design study*

Experiments in three rhesus macaques examined how stimulation/return-electrode location affects vestibulo-ocular response strength and alignment. Differences of 600–750 μm affected outcomes, illustrating why contact count alone cannot predict performance. [3](https://thecullenlab.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/01/s10162-025-01020-0.pdf)

### Progress to track: Functional response and benefit–risk

Compare eye-movement alignment, gait or symptom measures and hearing preservation. Distinguish the 10-person human cohort from the three-animal placement study. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10835607/) [3](https://thecullenlab.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/01/s10162-025-01020-0.pdf)

### Study context

The lab’s current trial page describes the device as investigational under an FDA device exemption, not approved for marketing, and provides information for two study cohorts. The undated page is a route to current trial contacts, not proof of a new efficacy result. [4](https://sites.google.com/site/vestibularneuroengineering/vestibular-implant-trial)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can stimulation selectivity and implantation technique preserve hearing while sustaining reliable balance and vision during everyday movement?

## Bio-Integrated Electronics and Systems Laboratory

**KAIST · South Korea**

Investigators: Jae-Woong Jeong

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation

Jae-Woong Jeong’s KAIST group creates soft, wireless and reconfigurable electronics for biological interfaces. Its current neural work spans animal optofluidic implants and minimally invasive electrical stimulation at the skin, alongside broader wearable and materials research. [1](https://www.jeongresearch.org/research) [4](https://www.jeongresearch.org/journals)

Flexible electronics, microfluidics, light-emitting probes and wireless control are combined to manipulate biological systems. Electrical stimulation, drug delivery and optogenetics are different modalities and should retain their own experimental evidence. [1](https://www.jeongresearch.org/research) [4](https://www.jeongresearch.org/journals)

**Methods:** Soft electronics; Microfluidic delivery; Optogenetics; Microneedle electrical stimulation; Wireless scheduling.

**Signals:** Light and drug delivery commands; Electrical stimulation; Physiological stress signals; Animal behavior.

### Projects

#### RAPIDO wireless optofluidic implant, 2026

*Preclinical rodent platform*

The July Science Advances paper integrates refillable, replaceable drug cartridges, backflow control, programmable pumping and a micro-LED probe. Experiments in freely behaving rodents demonstrate pharmacological modulation and optogenetic manipulation during cocaine conditioning. Internet scheduling supports observer-free experiments; it is not a human treatment result. [2](https://www.jeongresearch.org/s/78-IoT-enabled-neural-implant_SciAdv2026.pdf)

#### TEAM microneedle electroceutical, 2026

*Preclinical efficacy and human feasibility demonstration*

The August paper combines conductive adhesive microneedles, hydrogel and wireless control. Analgesic comparisons are preclinical; human work demonstrates physiological-stress-triggered stimulation as a proof of concept. It does not establish long-term treatment efficacy in chronic-pain patients. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-76527-y)

### Progress to track: Dosing, targeting and reliable control

For RAPIDO, relevant dimensions are fluid control, refilling and repeated multimodal operation. For TEAM, interface impedance and appropriate stimulation matter. Neither is a cortical recording-channel scaling result. [2](https://www.jeongresearch.org/s/78-IoT-enabled-neural-implant_SciAdv2026.pdf) [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-76527-y)

### Study context

These platforms support experimental neuroscience and investigation of connected stimulation systems. The primary papers distinguish animal validation from early human demonstrations and future translation. [2](https://www.jeongresearch.org/s/78-IoT-enabled-neural-implant_SciAdv2026.pdf) [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-76527-y)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: When softness, wireless operation, and multiple stimulation modes are combined, which component limits reliable long-term operation?

## Brain Interfaces and Neural Prosthetics Laboratory

**KAIST · South Korea**

Investigators: Seungwoo Lee

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation; Bioelectronics; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Seungwoo Lee’s Brain Interfaces and Neural Prosthetics laboratory at KAIST develops ways to stimulate the nervous system selectively. Its distinctive direction is implantable microcoils, alongside broader electrical stimulation and sensory-neuroprosthesis research. [1](https://bcs.kaist.ac.kr/sub020101/view/id/116) [4](https://binp.kaist.ac.kr/)

Changing current in a small coil creates an induced electric field that can influence nearby neurons. Coil geometry and field orientation can affect which neural processes are recruited; an implanted microcoil is different from noninvasive scalp TMS. [1](https://bcs.kaist.ac.kr/sub020101/view/id/116) [4](https://binp.kaist.ac.kr/)

**Methods:** Microcoil design; Electric/magnetic stimulation modeling; Neural stimulation; Electrophysiological validation.

**Signals:** Induced electric fields; Neuronal responses; Behavioral responses.

### Website review

Rewritten from freshly read official pages and identified research evidence. All retained claim sources were read at the stated scope.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.
