## Rice Brain Computer Interface Lab

**Rice University · United States**

Investigators: Nishal Shah

Areas: Neural decoding; Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation

Develops computational methods for reading intended movements and writing structured activity into neural circuits. [1](https://www.rice-npl.com/)

The lab connects motor-cortex decoding with population-level electrical stimulation. Its ambition is bidirectional interfacing: infer flexible actions from neural signals and select stimulation patterns that produce useful activity. [1](https://www.rice-npl.com/)

**Methods:** Intracortical decoding; Latent dynamical models; Optimized electrical stimulation; Virtual motor tasks.

**Signals:** Motor-cortex activity; Retinal population spikes; Attempted finger movements.

### Website review

Read the entire current lab page, including dated news and publications, and the May 2026 Rice/Baylor trial announcement.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [Rice and Baylor join BrainGate](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/clinical-trial-seeks-advance-intuitive-assistive-robotics-people-paralysis) — 2026-05-28.

- Updated motor and retinal studies to the journal dates listed by the lab while retaining typing/Contextual LFADS preprint status.
- Kept the newly announced clinical program separate from prior participant demonstrations.

- [Rice BCI Lab — projects, publications, and chronology](https://www.rice-npl.com/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Rice affiliation, January 2025 opening, 2025 journal/preprint chronology, retina and motor research.
- [Rice and Baylor join BrainGate](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/clinical-trial-seeks-advance-intuitive-assistive-robotics-people-paralysis) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. May 2026 BrainGate expansion, Rice engineering role and Baylor clinical role; planned daily-activity goals.

### Projects

#### Assistive robotic control at Rice and Baylor

*Human investigational research program*

In May 2026, Rice and Baylor announced joining BrainGate. Shah’s team develops decoding and robotic-control infrastructure, while Baylor provides clinical leadership. The stated focus includes coordinated actions for eating and drinking. [2](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/clinical-trial-seeks-advance-intuitive-assistive-robotics-people-paralysis)

#### Flexible motor representations

*Human participant demonstrations*

The lab lists a January 2025 Nature Medicine finger-decoding/quadcopter-game study and a May 2025 Nature Communications study of multi-finger neural geometry. A separate typing study remains labeled a 2024 preprint. These collaborative results are distinct from the new Rice clinical site. [1](https://www.rice-npl.com/)

#### Writing population activity in retina

*Ex-vivo retinal electrophysiology*

The publication list dates dynamically optimized electrical stimulation to eLife in November 2025. The lab describes an ex-vivo retinal prototype for producing target population activity; this does not demonstrate restored sight in a patient. [1](https://www.rice-npl.com/)

#### Contextual LFADS

*Preprint and research code, 2025*

The October 2025 lab announcement links a preprint and code for learning a context-dependent per-trial bias in LFADS. It aims to make latent dynamical models more interpretable; the announcement is not a human treatment outcome. [1](https://www.rice-npl.com/)

### Progress to track: Controllable movement dimensions and population fidelity

Useful scaling measures include independently decoded movements, task performance, and how well stimulation reproduces target activity. The number of electrodes alone does not specify those capabilities. [1](https://www.rice-npl.com/) [2](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/clinical-trial-seeks-advance-intuitive-assistive-robotics-people-paralysis)

### Study context

The Rice lab opened in January 2025; the local BrainGate expansion was announced in 2026. Separate the human motor-decoding studies, the new clinical program, and the ex-vivo retinal work when comparing maturity. [1](https://www.rice-npl.com/) [2](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/clinical-trial-seeks-advance-intuitive-assistive-robotics-people-paralysis)

### Editorial notebook question

Can a decoder support coordinated daily activities across changing contexts with less recalibration and assistance?

## Robinson Lab for Nano-neurotechnology

**Rice University · United States**

Investigators: Jacob Robinson

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neuromodulation; Neuroimaging; Neural interfaces

Builds miniature magnetic, electronic, and optical interfaces, alongside tools for observing neural circuits and behavior across species. [1](https://www.robinsonlab.com/research)

Magnetoelectric materials convert externally applied magnetic fields into electrical energy for small implants. A second thread co-designs optics and computation to collect neural and behavioral data with compact instruments. [1](https://www.robinsonlab.com/research)

**Methods:** Magnetoelectric power transfer; Battery-free stimulation; Endocisternal delivery; Lensless calcium imaging.

**Signals:** Neural electrical activity; Calcium fluorescence; Behavior.

### Website review

Read the research/publication pages and both complete device articles, including the May 2026 backscatter work.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [A roadmap to navigate the future of neural engineering — publication listing](https://www.robinsonlab.com/publications) — 2026-08.

- Specified sheep and the exact online date for endocisternal work.
- Added recent backscatter work with its cardiac, rather than neural, validation.
- Excluded the publication list’s post-cutoff November 2026 item.

- [Robinson Lab — current research](https://www.robinsonlab.com/research) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Magnetoelectric, lensless imaging and translational research directions; 2024 primate imaging citation.
- [Endocisternal neural interfaces](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-024-01281-9) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Full paper: November 2024 online date, sheep CSF-space delivery, recording/stimulation and explantation.
- [Robinson Lab — publications](https://www.robinsonlab.com/publications) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Dated 2026 publication list, including August roadmaps; future-dated entry excluded.
- [Robust magnetoelectric backscatter communication system for bioelectronic implants](https://www.nature.com/articles/s44172-026-00678-5) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Full May 2026 article: backscatter mechanism, receiver and porcine cardiac demonstration.

### Projects

#### Distributed wireless bioelectronics

*Published device and preclinical work*

Miniature stimulators and implant networks aim to reduce batteries, wiring, and implant size. The lab’s cited studies include epidural cortical stimulators and endovascular peripheral-nerve stimulation. These are distinct anatomical delivery routes. [1](https://www.robinsonlab.com/research)

#### Endocisternal access

*Preclinical interface study*

Published online in November 2024, this collaboration tested recording and stimulation through cerebrospinal-fluid spaces in sheep. It demonstrated navigation, repositioning and explantation after chronic implantation. These are preclinical delivery and interface results. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-024-01281-9)

#### NeuroAI measurement pipelines

*Nonhuman-primate imaging*

Lensless microscopes and behavioral pipelines support large neural datasets. A cited 2024 study demonstrates mesoscopic calcium imaging in a head-unrestrained male nonhuman primate, broadening measurement beyond tethered electrode arrays. [1](https://www.robinsonlab.com/research)

#### Magnetoelectric backscatter

*Engineering and porcine cardiac demonstration, 2026*

A May 2026 paper uses load modulation and an adaptive receiver to return information over a magnetoelectric link. Its physiological demonstration recorded electrical activity from a beating pig heart; it supports wireless bioelectronics development, not a demonstrated neural-recording implant. [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s44172-026-00678-5)

### Progress to track: Implant volume, wireless reach, and distributed access

Count independently powered and addressable implants alongside power delivery and recording or stimulation performance. For imaging, track field of view and behavioral freedom rather than electrode count. [1](https://www.robinsonlab.com/research) [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-024-01281-9)

### Study context

Therapeutic targets described by the lab are development goals. The cited endocisternal and imaging work should be interpreted in their preclinical settings, not as evidence that the full portfolio is an established treatment. [1](https://www.robinsonlab.com/research) [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-024-01281-9)

### Editorial notebook question

Can a network of small implants gain coverage while maintaining reliable power, synchronization, and selective control?

## Tringides Lab — Cell and Material Technologies

**Rice University · United States**

Investigators: Christina Tringides

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neural interfaces

Creates soft, electrically functional materials for conformable electrode arrays and laboratory models of nerves and neural disease. [1](https://profiles.rice.edu/faculty/christina-tringides)

The lab treats tissue mechanics as a design variable. Hydrogels with tunable electrical and viscoelastic properties support both implant structures and three-dimensional cultures that can be electrically measured. [1](https://profiles.rice.edu/faculty/christina-tringides)

**Methods:** Conductive hydrogels; Viscoelastic electrode arrays; Microfabricated culture channels; Multielectrode-array recording.

**Signals:** Culture electrophysiology; Signal conduction; Cell growth and myelination.

#### hydroMEA nerve model

*Human-derived cells in vitro, 2026*

The 2026 Rice–ETH study combines human-derived sensory neurons, Schwann cells, hydrogels, channels, and high-density MEA measurements. It tested functional myelination through faster electrical propagation rather than relying on appearance alone. [2](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/rice-researchers-build-3d-platform-study-how-nerves-form-protective-coating)

#### Neurons and glioblastoma in a controlled environment

*Planned Pew-funded research, announced June 2026*

The June 2026 Pew award report describes a planned model with neurons and glioblastoma cells in guided microstructures. The proposed experiments will measure invasion and electrical interactions while varying hydrogel properties; the award announcement does not report completed treatment results. [3](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/rices-tringides-wins-prestigious-pew-biomedical-scholar-award)

### Progress to track: Tissue compatibility and functional culture measurements

The hydroMEA report describes neuronal survival beyond 100 days and functional myelination measured electrically. This is an in-vitro culture duration, not the lifetime of an implanted BCI. [2](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/rice-researchers-build-3d-platform-study-how-nerves-form-protective-coating)

### Study context

Human-derived cells are not human clinical evidence. Both the nerve model and cancer platforms are tools to investigate mechanisms; clinical benefit from a hydrogel brain implant remains to be established. [2](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/rice-researchers-build-3d-platform-study-how-nerves-form-protective-coating) [3](https://news.rice.edu/news/2026/rices-tringides-wins-prestigious-pew-biomedical-scholar-award)

## Brain-like Computing and Machine Intelligence: EEG and Affective Computing

**Shanghai Jiao Tong University · China**

Investigators: Bao-Liang Lu; Wei-Long Zheng

Areas: Neural decoding; Computational neuroscience; Neural interfaces

The BCMI group studies how EEG and other physiological measurements reveal emotion, vigilance, and cognitive state. Its SEED dataset family is an important part of the research: experiments, labels, and benchmarks make algorithms comparable across people, sessions, and sensory contexts. [1](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/) [2](https://www.cs.sjtu.edu.cn/en/jiaoshiml/lyubaoliang.html)

The group combines representation learning with experimental protocols for eliciting and labeling mental states. A major engineering issue is distribution shift: a classifier trained on one person, session, or culture may fail elsewhere. EEG restoration and domain adaptation address different parts of this problem. [1](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/) [2](https://www.cs.sjtu.edu.cn/en/jiaoshiml/lyubaoliang.html)

**Methods:** EEG emotion-recognition experiments; Cross-subject and cross-session learning; Domain adaptation; Spatiotemporal autoencoders for signal restoration; Multitask EEG representation learning.

**Signals:** Scalp EEG; Eye-movement measurements; Emotion labels; Vigilance and driving-task measurements.

### Website review

Read the dataset index, full driving protocol and university research/publication profile through 2025.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [SEED dataset uptake snapshot](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/) — 2025-07.

- Corrected the real-world driving description to passenger-seat simulated control.
- Removed unsupported olfactory-dataset wording and added current listed representation-learning work.

- [SEED datasets and research team](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Lu/Zheng team identity, dataset families and July 2025 uptake snapshot.
- [Bao-Liang Lu university profile and publications](https://www.cs.sjtu.edu.cn/en/jiaoshiml/lyubaoliang.html) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. SJTU affiliation and 2024–2025 publication titles, including STAR and NeuroLM.
- [SEED-VLA vigilance dataset](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/seed-VLA.html) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. 20 laboratory and 14 passenger-seat participants, dry EEG, eye-closure labels and cited 2024 study.

### Projects

#### SEED emotion and vigilance resources

*Research datasets; driving study cited in 2024*

SEED distributes emotion, cross-cultural, sleep-condition, dynamic-visual and vigilance datasets. Its driving page distinguishes 20 laboratory participants from 14 participants in a moving car: the latter sat in the passenger seat using simulated controls while an experienced driver drove. Neither dataset is a real-world crash-prevention trial. [1](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/) [3](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/seed-VLA.html)

#### Restoring incomplete EEG

*2025 publication portfolio*

The university’s 2025 publication list includes STAR for EEG restoration, NeuroLM for multi-task EEG/language modeling, and cross-cultural adaptation work. These are distinct approaches to incomplete data and transfer; the listing alone does not establish comparative performance. [2](https://www.cs.sjtu.edu.cn/en/jiaoshiml/lyubaoliang.html)

### Progress to track: Generalization across participants and conditions

Dataset diversity and held-out participant performance are more appropriate measures here than electrode totals. The cited pages document multiple benchmark families; this profile does not combine their participants into a single experimental cohort. [1](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/) [3](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/seed-VLA.html)

### Study context

Vigilance monitoring, affect-aware interaction, and mental-health research motivate the work. An EEG emotion label is an experimental construct, not a direct or infallible reading of a person’s private feelings. [1](https://bcmi.sjtu.edu.cn/home/seed/) [2](https://www.cs.sjtu.edu.cn/en/jiaoshiml/lyubaoliang.html)

### Editorial notebook question

Can an affect decoder distinguish a robust physiological correlate from a shortcut tied to a particular film, participant, or recording session?

## Brains in Silicon Laboratory

**Stanford University · United States**

Investigators: Kwabena Boahen

Areas: Computational neuroscience

Kwabena Boahen’s group builds computing hardware inspired by neural biophysics. Its subject is efficient computation and models of brain circuits, rather than implantable electrode arrays. The lab connects transistor physics, circuit architecture, neuroscience, and the software needed to program analog hardware. [1](https://profiles.stanford.edu/kwabena-boahen) [4](https://web.stanford.edu/group/brainsinsilicon/projects/)

Mixed analog and digital circuits use device dynamics to implement neural computations, while event-based communication moves activity between components. The central question is how to obtain useful, programmable computation without paying the energy and data-movement costs of conventional arithmetic at every simulated time step. [1](https://profiles.stanford.edu/kwabena-boahen) [4](https://web.stanford.edu/group/brainsinsilicon/projects/)

**Methods:** Mixed-signal CMOS design; Event-based spike communication; Dynamical-systems programming; Biophysical and network simulation.

**Signals:** Synthetic spike events; Analog circuit states; Computational model outputs.

### Website review

Read the Stanford faculty profile, live projects, original Neurogrid PDF and Braindrop abstract; explicitly searched 2024–2026 updates.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [A Low Thermal Sensitivity Subthreshold-Current to Pulse-Frequency Converter for Neuromorphic Chips — latest faculty-listed paper](https://profiles.stanford.edu/kwabena-boahen) — 2023.

- Dated Neurogrid and Braindrop as landmarks.
- Added the current dendritic/sparse-coding direction with a clear proposal-versus-result boundary.
- Removed the Bioelectronics tag to keep synthetic neuromorphic computing distinct from interfaces to living tissue in this directory.

- [Kwabena Boahen: Brains in Silicon research and translation](https://profiles.stanford.edu/kwabena-boahen) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Current affiliation, laboratory mission, 2018 Femtosense spinout and latest listed 2023 papers.
- [Neurogrid: A Mixed-Analog-Digital Multichip System for Large-Scale Neural Simulations](https://web.stanford.edu/group/brainsinsilicon/documents/BenjaminEtAlNeurogrid2014.pdf) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. PDF first page: 2014 publication date and mixed analog/digital multichip architecture.
- [Braindrop: A Mixed-Signal Neuromorphic Architecture With a Dynamical Systems-Based Programming Model](https://csl.yale.edu/~rajit/abstracts/braindrop.html) — Abstract / publication record read; checked 2026-09-15. Dynamical-systems programming, analog mismatch compensation and 4,096 artificial neurons.
- [Brains in Silicon — current projects](https://web.stanford.edu/group/brainsinsilicon/projects/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Undated current proposals for sparse codes, dendritic computing and event-based vision sensors.

### Projects

#### Neurogrid

*Research hardware landmark, 2014*

The multichip Neurogrid platform emulates large neural populations with analog neural dynamics and digital communication. It provides a hardware substrate for investigating neural circuit organization and efficient large-scale simulation. [2](https://web.stanford.edu/group/brainsinsilicon/documents/BenjaminEtAlNeurogrid2014.pdf)

#### Braindrop and hardware–software co-design

*Research chip landmark, 2019*

Braindrop links a mixed-signal neuromorphic architecture to a dynamical-systems programming model. The aim is to make heterogeneous analog circuitry usable through higher-level descriptions instead of requiring users to tune every device by hand. [3](https://csl.yale.edu/~rajit/abstracts/braindrop.html)

#### Sparse codes and dendritic computing

*Current project description; undated*

The live project page describes sparse spatiotemporal coding, dendrite-inspired computation and stacked retinomorphic sensors. It proposes reducing communication and thermal costs through circuit organization. The page is undated, so its goals are not presented as a newly measured 2026 system result. [4](https://web.stanford.edu/group/brainsinsilicon/projects/)

### Progress to track: Emulated neurons, programming, and energy

Braindrop integrates 4,096 artificial neurons. These are circuit elements, not recorded biological cells or BCI channels. Useful scaling also depends on the supported computation, communication overhead, and the energy required to execute a task. [3](https://csl.yale.edu/~rajit/abstracts/braindrop.html)

### Study context

Stanford identifies the Brainstorm hardware–software effort and the 2018 Femtosense spinout as translation routes. This is a computing commercialization pathway, distinct from medical-device validation or demonstrated treatment of neurological disease. [1](https://profiles.stanford.edu/kwabena-boahen)

### Editorial notebook question

Which biologically inspired dynamics remain useful when evaluated on an end-to-end workload that includes data movement and programming overhead?

## Neural Prosthetics Translational Laboratory

**Stanford University · United States**

Investigators: Jaimie Henderson; Frank Willett

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Computational neuroscience; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

NPTL connects human motor and language neuroscience to assistive communication. The current laboratory identifies Jaimie Henderson and Frank Willett as co-directors. Its history includes Krishna Shenoy, who died in 2023; he should not be presented as a current director. [1](https://nptl.stanford.edu/)

The team records intracortical population activity while participants with paralysis attempt movements or speech, then builds real-time decoders around those representations. Its research combines dimensionality reduction, neural dynamics, recurrent networks, and clinical system design. Automatic recalibration addresses changing neural signals between sessions. [1](https://nptl.stanford.edu/)

**Methods:** Utah microelectrode arrays; Recurrent neural network decoding; Population dynamics and dimensionality reduction; Automatic decoder recalibration.

**Signals:** Intracortical spiking activity; Local field potentials; Attempted speech and movement labels.

### Website review

Read the live leadership/research page, publication index, 2023 speech article and 2026 motor-map paper abstract.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [A mosaic of whole-body representations on the human precentral gyrus](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-10653-x) — 2026-06-17.

- Confirmed Henderson and Willett as current co-directors and Shenoy as historical leadership.
- Added the 2026 multi-participant map, preserving its array/participant denominator.
- Kept the 2023 speech speed tied to its vocabulary-dependent error.

- [Stanford NPTL: leadership, research, and translation](https://nptl.stanford.edu/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Current co-directors, Shenoy legacy, BrainGate2 scope and motor/communication research.
- [A high-performance speech neuroprosthesis](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-023-06377-x) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Full 2023 paper, one ALS participant, 62 words/minute and vocabulary-dependent errors.
- [NPTL — journal papers](https://nptl.stanford.edu/journal-papers) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. 2025–2026 publication chronology; older preprint versions remain separately listed.
- [A mosaic of whole-body representations on the human precentral gyrus](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-10653-x) — Abstract / publication record read; checked 2026-09-15. June 2026 online date, 20 arrays across eight participants and intermixed body representations.

### Projects

#### Attempted speech to text

*Human clinical-research demonstration, 2023*

The 2023 speech neuroprosthesis decoded attempted speech in a participant with ALS. The system combined neural decoding with language modeling, making communication speed and vocabulary-dependent error separate performance dimensions. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-023-06377-x)

#### Attempted handwriting and flexible movement control

*Human clinical research and algorithm development*

The laboratory studies distributed movement representations and translates attempted handwriting into characters. Related finger-control research tests richer digital interaction, while calibration work asks how a learned mapping survives changes in the recorded population. [1](https://nptl.stanford.edu/)

#### Mapping movement across the precentral gyrus

*Multi-participant human research, 2026*

The June 2026 paper analyzes recordings from 20 microelectrode arrays across eight participants with paralysis. Body-part representations were intermixed across sampled locations, with differing relative strengths. This pooled anatomical coverage informs BCI targeting; it is not 20 arrays in one participant or a simultaneous whole-brain recording. [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-10653-x)

### Progress to track: Communication performance and robustness

The 2023 study reported 62 words per minute. That behavioral throughput must not be equated with electrode count, independent neurons, or error-free conversation; accuracy depends on the vocabulary and decoding configuration. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-023-06377-x)

### Study context

This is a BrainGate2 clinical-research program for people with severe motor impairment. Translation includes real-time use, signal drift, and participant priorities, rather than an established broadly available clinical product. [1](https://nptl.stanford.edu/) [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-023-06377-x)

### Editorial notebook question

Can automatic adaptation preserve communication quality for months without requiring users to repeat lengthy calibration tasks?

2. [A high-performance speech neuroprosthesis](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-023-06377-x) — Paper; 2023-08-23. Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Full 2023 paper, one ALS participant, 62 words/minute and vocabulary-dependent errors.

## Neural Interfacing Lab

**Technical University of Munich · Germany**

Investigators: Melissa Zavaglia

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

TUM’s Neural Interfacing Lab connects biological signal acquisition with models, robots and assistive devices. The MIRMI laboratory page identifies Melissa Zavaglia as senior scientist and describes a facility under development. Its distinctive contribution is an integrated experimental workflow across sensing, processing and device control. [1](https://www.mirmi.tum.de/mirmi/forschung/labore/neural-interfacing/)

The live page names Melissa Zavaglia as senior scientist and Ioannis Xygonakis as lab manager. It describes EEG, high-density EMG, neural implants, in-vitro sensor testing, signal modeling and stimulation as parts of an integrated pipeline. Its under-development wording repeats the February 2024 announcement; it does not provide a dated 2026 completion milestone. [1](https://www.mirmi.tum.de/mirmi/forschung/labore/neural-interfacing/)

**Methods:** EEG acquisition; High-density EMG; In-vitro sensor testing; Signal-processing pipelines; Assistive robot integration; Neural stimulation.

### Projects

#### Sensor-to-model experimental pipeline

*Laboratory platform development*

The laboratory description covers selecting and testing sensors, placing them appropriately and extracting useful information from their signals. This provides a shared route for comparing interface options before connecting decoded information to an assistive system. [1](https://www.mirmi.tum.de/mirmi/forschung/labore/neural-interfacing/)

#### Interfaces for neurodeficit assistance

*Assistive-interface research programme*

The 2024 institutional profile links the lab with AI.D, Artificial Intelligence for Neuro Deficits, and describes brain-machine-interface research connected to robotic and assistive devices. ## Neuroelectronics Group

**Technical University of Munich · Germany**

Investigators: Bernhard Wolfrum

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neural interfaces

Wolfrum’s Neuroelectronics group engineers interfaces for recording and stimulating biological systems, including chemical as well as electrical communication. At TUM’s Garching campus, fabrication research connects printed electronics and sensor arrays with controlled cellular preparations and neural-interface development. [1](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/home/)

The programme uses structured cultures to make biological organization part of the experiment. Microchannels can orient axons, while localized lesions probe how network structure affects signalling and repair. Printed and three-dimensional electrodes address the geometry of tissue contact rather than simply expanding planar site counts. [1](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/home/)

**Methods:** Printed microelectrode arrays; Three-dimensional electrode fabrication; Microchannel-guided cultures; Localized axotomy; Electrical recording and stimulation; Neurotransmitter sensing.

**Signals:** Extracellular electrical activity; Synaptic neurotransmitter release; Activity in cultured tissue and organoids.

### Projects

#### Three-dimensional interfaces for organoids

*In-vitro device study, 2026*

A 2026 paper describes porous, well-shaped arrays made with conformal coatings, laser ablation and 3D-printed forms. The wells physically retain cortical organoids over the electrodes; the study reports extracellular field potentials from organoids and cardiac cell layers. This is in-vitro access, not a human neural implant. [3](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/publications/) [5](https://push-zb.helmholtz-munich.de/frontdoor.php?la=de&source_opus=77331)

#### Structured networks and axotomy

*In-vitro network and injury models*

The group develops microwire arrays for localized lesions and microchannels that guide axons, with a future goal of combined regeneration models. Its structured-network page also discusses cardiomyocyte experiments, which must be distinguished from the neuronal work. [2](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/research/structured-cell-networks/)

### Progress to track: Contact geometry and controlled biological access

Useful progress includes reaching three-dimensional tissue, localizing perturbations and interpreting chemical or electrical responses. The cited pages do not provide a consistent electrode-count history across these platforms. [1](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/home/) [2](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/research/structured-cell-networks/) [3](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/publications/) [4](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/ee/forschung/forschungsgruppen/)

### Study context

The group spans in-vitro and in-vivo interface engineering. Research prototypes and public demonstrations do not by themselves establish clinical safety or therapeutic benefit. [1](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/home/) [2](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/research/structured-cell-networks/) [3](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/nel/publications/) [4](https://www.ee.cit.tum.de/ee/forschung/forschungsgruppen/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can printed three-dimensional interfaces improve longitudinal organoid measurements without changing tissue development or obscuring which cells generate each signal?

## Neurotechnology / Berlin Brain-Computer Interface

**Technische Universität Berlin · Germany**

Investigators: Benjamin Blankertz

Areas: Neural decoding; Neural interfaces; Computational neuroscience

TU Berlin’s Neurotechnology group develops brain-computer interfaces and human-computer interaction methods through advanced data analysis. Its current chair is Benjamin Blankertz. The Berlin Brain-Computer Interface provides historical and collaborative context, but old BBCI partner lists should not be treated as a current roster of European laboratories. [1](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro)

EEG sensing and machine learning connect experimental brain research with practical interfaces. An important engineering challenge is coadaptation: both the user and the algorithm change while the interface is being learned. Calibration design therefore matters alongside classifier accuracy. [1](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro)

**Methods:** EEG decoding; Active learning; Sequential learning; Adaptive classification; Information-seeking experimental design; Data-driven head-model individualization.

**Signals:** EEG; Task-related brain potentials; User responses and interaction behaviour.

January 2026 date, scalp-derived head models, 16-person anatomy evaluation and 22-head simulation.

### Projects

#### DAEDALUS coadaptation project

*Undated graduate-school project description*

The graduate-school project on active learning for better user–BCI coadaptation investigates how sequential and active learning can reduce calibration burden. It considers information in EEG while users inspect stimuli, including signals that may be available before an explicit response. [4](https://www.tu.berlin/plastro/graduate-school-daedalus/projects)

#### EEG sensing and decoding programme

*Research agenda and undated project index*

The current research page focuses on EEG sensors and machine learning. The project index lists MindSee, BCI Live and other initiatives without dates or completion labels; those entries alone do not establish present activity or recruitment. [2](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro/research) [3](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro/research/projects)

#### Individualized head models without a new MRI

*Human anatomy evaluation and EEG simulation, 2026*

The January 2026 PCAwarp study fits a statistical anatomy model using electrode positions or scalp photogrammetry. It evaluates anatomy approximation in 16 participants and EEG source localization in simulations using 22 heads. Those are separate evaluations, not 38 clinical BCI users. [6](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41523215/)

### Progress to track: Calibration burden and useful interaction

Time needed to establish reliable control, adaptation across sessions and effective user interaction are meaningful measures. Increasing scalp channels alone does not demonstrate progress on these outcomes. [1](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro) [2](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro/research) [3](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro/research/projects) [4](https://www.tu.berlin/plastro/graduate-school-daedalus/projects)

### Study context

The programme can inform assistive access and broader human-computer interaction. Clinical effectiveness and product readiness need application-specific evidence beyond an institutional list of BCI projects. [1](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro) [2](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro/research) [3](https://www.tu.berlin/en/neuro/research/projects) [4](https://www.tu.berlin/plastro/graduate-school-daedalus/projects)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can active calibration reduce setup time without allowing user and decoder adaptation to reinforce misleading or unstable EEG features?

## Eldawlatly Neural Engineering Research

**The American University in Cairo · Egypt**

Investigators: Seif Eldawlatly

Areas: Neural decoding; Neural interfaces; Computational neuroscience; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Eldawlatly’s university-described research uses signal processing and machine learning for invasive and noninvasive interfaces, visual prostheses, and neurological applications. This profile represents a faculty research program; the university page does not establish a separate formal laboratory name. [1](https://www.aucegypt.edu/fac/seif-eldawlatly) [2](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39029497/)

The work links algorithms to interface constraints: motor-imagery decoders must transfer between people, while prosthetic-vision processing must simplify a scene without discarding the information a user needs. The portfolio also includes EMG-based movement decoding and neural visual-encoding models. [1](https://www.aucegypt.edu/fac/seif-eldawlatly) [2](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39029497/)

**Methods:** EEG time-frequency representation; Convolutional neural networks; Wasserstein generative adversarial networks; Cross-subject validation; Image simplification for prosthetic vision.

**Signals:** Motor-imagery EEG; EMG; Visual images and neural visual responses.

### Website review

Read the faculty biography/publications, July 2024 paper abstract and Winter 2025 university feature; searched 2026 updates without establishing a newer BCI result.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [Mind Over Matter — Winter 2025 university feature](https://auctoday.aucegypt.edu/news/mind-over-matter) — 2025.

- Added explicit evidence that the reported invasive work was animal-only as of the 2025 feature.
- Kept faculty research-program identity and avoided conflicting rank wording across current and historical pages.

- [AUC Seif Eldawlatly faculty profile and publications](https://www.aucegypt.edu/fac/seif-eldawlatly) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. AUC affiliation, research-program scope and dated 2022–2024 publications.
- [Toward calibration-free motor imagery brain-computer interfaces: a VGG-based convolutional neural network and WGAN approach](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39029497/) — Abstract / publication record read; checked 2026-09-15. Modified VGG/WGAN method, three benchmark datasets and leave-one-subject-out evaluation.
- [Mind Over Matter — AUCToday](https://auctoday.aucegypt.edu/news/mind-over-matter) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Winter 2025 feature distinguishes animal invasive work from human scalp-EEG demonstrations.

### Projects

#### Reducing motor-imagery calibration

*Published benchmark analysis, July 2024*

A 2024 Journal of Neural Engineering study combines a modified VGG classifier with WGAN-generated spectral images. It evaluates transfer using leave-one-subject-out analysis on BCI Competition IV-2B, IV-2A, and IV-1, avoiding target-subject calibration data within that evaluation. [2](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39029497/)

#### Making images useful for prosthetic vision

*Published image-processing research*

The faculty portfolio lists PVGAN, a 2022 generative model for object simplification in prosthetic vision. This asks how to encode useful visual structure under a constrained display rather than assuming more image detail is always better. [1](https://www.aucegypt.edu/fac/seif-eldawlatly)

### Progress to track: Cross-subject generalization across three benchmarks

The three-dataset evaluation is a reproducible algorithmic benchmark. It is not a count of prospective clinical trials, and benchmark transfer does not establish reliable daily operation for every user. [2](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39029497/)

### Study context

The Winter 2025 university feature explicitly says Eldawlatly’s invasive procedures had been limited to animal testing. It separately describes noninvasive human EEG projects. Neither the image-processing studies nor benchmark transfer establishes a human visual-implant trial. [1](https://www.aucegypt.edu/fac/seif-eldawlatly) [2](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39029497/) [3](https://auctoday.aucegypt.edu/news/mind-over-matter)

### Editorial notebook question

Will a calibration-free model remain reliable when the device, task instructions, and user population differ from the training benchmarks?

## Gao Brain–Computer Interface Research

**Tsinghua University · China**

Investigators: Xiaorong Gao

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Gao’s Tsinghua program links visual EEG communication, decoding algorithms and interface hardware. Its newer portfolio also includes a ventricular implant collaboration in animals, so the program spans noninvasive and invasive research with different evidence requirements. [2](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1073/1131.htm) [3](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1051/1207.htm)

Visual interfaces co-design stimulus timing, signal processing and feedback to recover intended commands from scalp EEG. In the separate 2026 animal study, the engineering intervention is a flexible expandable electrode placed in a cerebrospinal-fluid cavity to reduce mechanical coupling with brain tissue. [2](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1073/1131.htm) [3](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1051/1207.htm)

**Methods:** Steady-state visually evoked potential stimulation; Joint spatial, temporal, and frequency feature extraction; EEG acquisition electronics; EEG neurofeedback; Expandable ventricular electrodes.

**Signals:** Scalp EEG; Steady-state visually evoked potentials; Ventricular field potentials in animal experiments.

### Projects

#### High-speed visual spelling

*Historical human spelling experiment, 2015*

The university profile describes a 2015 spelling interface that combined visual stimulation and decoding to reach 267 bits per minute. This is a historical task-specific information-transfer result, not a general-purpose neural bandwidth measurement. [2](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1073/1131.htm)

#### EEG-guided robotic interaction

*Dated EEG research portfolio, 2019–2025*

The earlier portfolio includes visual BCI combined with computer vision for robotic-arm control (2019). The updated faculty page lists 2024 dual-frequency SSVEP work and 2025 EEG representation and information-fusion studies; those titles do not alone establish everyday autonomous use. [2](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1073/1131.htm)

#### Lantern-inspired ventricular interface

*Preclinical implant study, 2026*

The March 2026 university report describes a February 2026 paper comparing an expandable ventricular electrode with same-material cortical electrodes in animals. It reports months-long signal assessment and memory-guided T-maze decoding. Adaptation to human ventricular anatomy and long-term safety remain future work. [3](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1051/1207.htm)

### Progress to track: Communication throughput

The cited 267 bits/min result belongs to the 2015 spelling experiment; the profile also records a 64-channel EEG acquisition system used in a 2010 competition. Channels and information-transfer rate measure different things. [2](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1073/1131.htm)

### Study context

Human visual-EEG studies and the ventricular animal experiment represent separate translation paths. The 2026 report does not establish a human ventricular implant, and broad school-wide clinical achievements are not attributed to Gao’s group without project-specific evidence. [2](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1073/1131.htm) [1](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/Research1/Neuro_engineering_and_Intelligent_Medicine.htm) [3](https://bme.tsinghua.edu.cn/en/info/1051/1207.htm)

### Editorial notebook question

How much communication speed survives when stimulus fatigue, calibration time, and operation outside a laboratory are included?

## Neural Computation Laboratory

**University College London · United Kingdom**

Investigators: Beverley Clark; Arnd Roth; Michael Häusser (honorary)

Areas: Computational neuroscience; Neuroimaging; Neural interfaces

The Neural Computation Laboratory studies how synapses, dendrites and circuit wiring transform incoming signals into neuronal output. UCL currently lists Beverley Clark, Arnd Roth and honorary professor Michael Häusser. Its cerebellar and neocortical research connects cell physiology with theories of computation. [1](https://www.ucl.ac.uk/medical-sciences/divisions/wolfson-institute-biomedical-research/research/neural-computation)

The group combines recordings from individual cells and dendrites with two-photon imaging, optogenetics, anatomical analysis and computational models. Optical stimulation supplies a controlled input; electrical or optical measurements reveal the response. This makes the laboratory especially useful for learning how causal circuit experiments complement large population recordings. [1](https://www.ucl.ac.uk/medical-sciences/divisions/wolfson-institute-biomedical-research/research/neural-computation)

**Methods:** Two-photon voltage imaging; Optogenetic input activation; Dendritic patch-clamp recordings; Neuropixels recordings; Cell and network modelling.

**Signals:** Membrane-voltage fluorescence; Subthreshold synaptic potentials; Action potentials; Purkinje-cell complex spikes.

### Website review

Retained claims rebuilt from the listed pages. Recent publications or news were sought explicitly; dated examples are identified in the profile.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [All-optical voltage interrogation for probing synaptic plasticity in vivo](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-63867-4) — 2025-10-03.

- Re-read the UCL programme and 2025 paper.
- Removed the isolated 440-Hz figure rather than presenting it as laboratory-wide scale.
- Kept the honorary status explicit.

- [Neural Computation](https://www.ucl.ac.uk/medical-sciences/divisions/wolfson-institute-biomedical-research/research/neural-computation) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Read UCL programme, methods and named group leaders through the web reader.
- [All-optical voltage interrogation for probing synaptic plasticity in vivo](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-63867-4) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Read abstract and experimental description; supports the 2025 awake-mouse optical plasticity example.

### Projects

#### Reading and perturbing synaptic responses, 2025

*Published awake-mouse experiments*

A collaboration combined the JEDI-2Psub voltage indicator with optogenetic activation of granule cells in awake mice. It measured subthreshold and suprathreshold responses in Purkinje-cell dendrites. Pairing granule-cell activation with sensory-evoked climbing-fibre input produced lasting changes in inhibitory responses. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-63867-4)

#### Dendrites and network computation

*Current basic-research programme*

The lab programme asks how the integration of inputs within a dendrite and the arrangement of connections between cells shape computation in cerebellum and neocortex. Models provide testable explanations for the physiological measurements. [1](https://www.ucl.ac.uk/medical-sciences/divisions/wolfson-institute-biomedical-research/research/neural-computation)

### Progress to track: Subcellular access and response sensitivity

Here progress concerns resolving synaptic responses while controlling their inputs. An imaged dendrite, a fluorescent voltage sample and an electrode channel are different measurement units; they should not be pooled into one scaling curve. [1](https://www.ucl.ac.uk/medical-sciences/divisions/wolfson-institute-biomedical-research/research/neural-computation) [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-63867-4)

### Study context

The cited work provides tools for investigating plasticity in behaving animals. Its demonstrated outcome is a measurable circuit response, rather than a clinical communication interface. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-025-63867-4)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can the same identified inputs and dendrites be followed through learning without confusing plasticity with a change in behavioural state?

## Neuroengineering Group, Institute of Biomedical Engineering

**University of Buenos Aires · Argentina**

Investigators: Sergio Lew

Areas: Computational neuroscience; Neural decoding; Neural interfaces

The Neuroengineering Group sits within the University of Buenos Aires Faculty of Engineering’s biomedical engineering institute. Its official roster identifies Sergio Lew as responsible for the group. The institute links animal neurophysiology, neural interfaces and rehabilitation engineering. [1](https://www.fi.uba.ar/investigacion/areas-de-investigacion/bioingenieria/grupo-de-neuroingenieria/integrantes) [2](https://www.fi.uba.ar/institucional/institutos-centros-y-escuelas/instituto-de-ingenieria-biomedica)

The institute describes analysis of frontal, motor and dopaminergic neural activity to understand decisions and behaviour and to inform neuroprostheses. A useful complementary strand is computational analysis of clinical recordings: it asks what a network-level quantity can reveal that a single electrode trace cannot. [1](https://www.fi.uba.ar/investigacion/areas-de-investigacion/bioingenieria/grupo-de-neuroingenieria/integrantes) [2](https://www.fi.uba.ar/institucional/institutos-centros-y-escuelas/instituto-de-ingenieria-biomedica)

**Methods:** Animal neural-activity analysis; Computational modelling of neural networks; Stereoelectroencephalography analysis; Quantitative comparison with behavioural scores.

**Signals:** Neuronal activity in frontal and motor systems; Intracranial EEG; Seizure-phase network measures; Clinical consciousness scores.

### Projects

#### Integrated information during seizures, 2024

*Conference abstract; exploratory human analysis*

A conference abstract associated with Lew analysed stereoelectroencephalography from six people with refractory epilepsy. It compared an autoregressive integrated-information measure across interictal, preictal and ictal periods, using contacts outside the epileptogenic region, and examined its association with consciousness scores. [3](https://csan2024.saneurociencias.org.ar/s6-neural-activity-and-behavior-in-vertebrates-the-spark-that-moves-us/)

#### From motor and decision circuits to interfaces

*Institutional research programme*

The institute describes basic neuroscience and neuroengineering work on animal frontal, motor and dopaminergic neurons as a basis for neural interfaces and neuroprostheses. This is the documented programme scope; the current-projects page does not supply a dated project inventory. [2](https://www.fi.uba.ar/institucional/institutos-centros-y-escuelas/instituto-de-ingenieria-biomedica)

### Progress to track: Network state and clinical correspondence

The six-person 2024 abstract is a cohort denominator, not the number of implanted probes or independently decoded neurons. The main comparison is between seizure phases and clinical observations. [3](https://csan2024.saneurociencias.org.ar/s6-neural-activity-and-behavior-in-vertebrates-the-spark-that-moves-us/)

### Study context

The group bridges computational neurophysiology and biomedical engineering. The conference analysis is evidence of an investigational biomarker approach; it does not establish a diagnostic device or treatment effect. [2](https://www.fi.uba.ar/institucional/institutos-centros-y-escuelas/instituto-de-ingenieria-biomedica) [3](https://csan2024.saneurociencias.org.ar/s6-neural-activity-and-behavior-in-vertebrates-the-spark-that-moves-us/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Does the network measure generalize to unseen patients and add information beyond conventional seizure and consciousness measures?

## Muller Lab

**University of California, Berkeley · United States**

Investigators: Rikky Muller

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation

Rikky Muller’s Berkeley group builds low-power electronics that make neural interfaces usable as complete systems: sensors, amplification, stimulation, signal processing and wireless communication. The research spans implantable interfaces and wearable physiological sensing, with energy and interference often more important than raw contact count. [1](https://www2.eecs.berkeley.edu/Faculty/Homepages/rikky.html) [4](https://www.rikkymuller.com/publications)

The group designs integrated circuits together with the communication link and experimental use case. Its older WAND system tackles the large electrical artifacts produced by stimulation; newer DustNet work tackles how several tiny implants can share a single ultrasonic link. [1](https://www2.eecs.berkeley.edu/Faculty/Homepages/rikky.html) [4](https://www.rikkymuller.com/publications)

**Methods:** Custom mixed-signal integrated circuits; Stimulation-artifact cancellation; Ultrasonic power and backscatter; Time-division multiple access; Wireless physiological monitoring.

**Signals:** Local field potentials; Peripheral neural recordings; EEG and other biosignals; Ultrasonic backscatter data.

### Website review

Retained claims rebuilt from the listed pages. Recent publications or news were sought explicitly; dated examples are identified in the profile.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Newest dated source found: [DustNet: A Wireless Network of Ultrasonic Neural Implants, revised version](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.14986) — 2026-04-26.

- Replaced the older ear-EEG example with the 2025–2026 DustNet preprint.
- Separated implant nodes, channels and bench link validation.
- Retained WAND as a 2018 landmark with experimental context.

### Projects

#### DustNet wireless implant network, 2025–2026

*Preprint; revised 26 April 2026; bench validation*

The authors’ preprint describes support for up to eight recording nodes on one ultrasound link, using time-division access and multilevel backscatter. The reported system validation includes a 90-mm link in oil. This is a communications and circuit demonstration, not evidence of an eight-node chronic human implant. [3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.14986)

#### WAND artifact-resistant closed loop, 2018

*Published non-human-primate demonstration*

WAND combined 128 recording channels with stimulation and on-board artifact handling. Non-human-primate experiments used local field potentials and task-related stimulation to demonstrate closed-loop operation. It remains a dated example of why stimulation compatibility changes usable recording performance. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-018-0323-x)

### Progress to track: Usable simultaneous links and recording during stimulation

WAND’s 128 recording channels and DustNet’s up-to-eight network nodes count different parts of a system. Link throughput, power and recovery from stimulation artifacts determine which signals can be used in real time. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-018-0323-x) [3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.14986)

### Study context

The current publication list includes 2026 bio-signal circuit work such as ReFIND. These are engineering research platforms; each application needs its own biological and chronic-use validation. [4](https://www.rikkymuller.com/publications) [3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.14986)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can more wireless nodes be added while preserving power margin, data reliability and chronic recording quality?

## UC Davis Neuroprosthetics Laboratory

**University of California, Davis · United States**

Investigators: Sergey Stavisky; David Brandman

Areas: Neural decoding; Neural interfaces; Computational neuroscience; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

The UC Davis Neuroprosthetics Laboratory is co-led by Sergey Stavisky and David Brandman. It combines neurosurgery, intracortical recording and machine learning to restore communication and computer access for people with severe paralysis. Recent work emphasizes independent everyday use as well as decoding performance. [1](https://health.ucdavis.edu/neurological-surgery/research/labs/neuroprosthetics)

The lab studies speech-related population activity with implanted arrays and turns it into text, cursor commands or synthesized voice. Its clinical experiments include repeated calibration and evaluation with an individual participant, so usability, self-paced operation and durability matter alongside prompted-task accuracy. [1](https://health.ucdavis.edu/neurological-surgery/research/labs/neuroprosthetics)

**Methods:** Intracortical population recording; Speech and cursor decoding; Streaming voice synthesis; Longitudinal home-use evaluation; Participant-rated communication assessment.

**Signals:** Extracellular motor-cortex signals; Attempted-speech activity; Cursor-control commands; Synthesized speech and decoded text.

### Projects

#### Independent communication and cursor use, 2026

*Published single-participant longitudinal study*

A Nature Medicine report follows one participant with ALS over nearly two years. The participant used the system independently for more than 3,800 hours and generated approximately two million words. The paper separates self-reported everyday sentence correctness from formal prompted-task accuracy. [4](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13375540/)

#### Instantaneous voice synthesis, 2025

*Published single-participant clinical research*

A Nature study used 256 microelectrodes in ventral precentral cortex to synthesize voice during attempted speech in one participant with ALS. The system also supported aspects of expressive speech, including intonation, and demonstrated short melodies. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-025-09127-3)

#### Rapid speech-decoder calibration, 2024

*Published clinical feasibility study*

An earlier report used four arrays with 256 electrodes and progressively expanded the decoding vocabulary. It provides the dated baseline for the subsequent longitudinal programme rather than a separate large clinical cohort. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11328962/)

### Progress to track: Independent use over time

The 2026 study reports more than 3,800 hours of independent use. That duration measures practical use by one person; it should be read alongside error measures, calibration needs and the specific interface hardware. [4](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13375540/)

### Study context

These are substantial longitudinal clinical demonstrations. They remain participant-specific research results; performance across a larger population and routine service delivery are separate questions. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-025-09127-3) [4](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13375540/)

## Neuroelectronics Lab

**University of California, San Diego · United States**

Investigators: Duygu Kuzum

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neuroimaging; Bioelectronics

Duygu Kuzum’s UC San Diego Neuroelectronics Lab links neural interfaces with energy-efficient computing. One branch develops transparent, multimodal tools to observe biological circuits; another builds electronic materials and circuits inspired by neural computation. The two branches have different experimental endpoints. [1](https://neuroelectronics.ucsd.edu/) [2](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/transparent-brain-implant-can-read-deep-neural-activity-from-the-surface) [3](https://neuroelectronics.ucsd.edu/publications-2/) [4](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/brain-inspired-device-could-lead-to-faster-more-energy-efficient-ai-hardware)

Transparent graphene electrodes allow electrical recordings and optical imaging to interrogate overlapping tissue. In the computing programme, resistive materials store information and transform inputs in the same physical network, reducing the need to move data between separate memory and processing units. [1](https://neuroelectronics.ucsd.edu/) [2](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/transparent-brain-implant-can-read-deep-neural-activity-from-the-surface) [3](https://neuroelectronics.ucsd.edu/publications-2/) [4](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/brain-inspired-device-could-lead-to-faster-more-energy-efficient-ai-hardware)

**Methods:** Transparent graphene microelectrodes; Two-photon calcium imaging; Cross-modal neural decoding; Resistive-memory devices; Neuromorphic material-network modelling.

**Signals:** Surface electrical activity; Calcium fluorescence; Device resistance and ionic dynamics; EEG datasets used for computational evaluation.

### Projects

#### Inferring deeper activity from a transparent surface interface, 2024

*Published mouse tool and modelling study*

The mouse study combined a transparent cortical-surface array with two-photon imaging. A neural network learned relationships between surface electrical activity and calcium activity at deeper imaging planes. The inferred signal is a model prediction supported by paired measurements, not direct electrical recording of each deeper neuron. [2](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/transparent-brain-implant-can-read-deep-neural-activity-from-the-surface)

#### Protonic nickelate computing, 2026

*Early hardware experiments and simulation*

UC San Diego describes a hydrogen-doped nickelate platform in which connected nodes interact through a shared material. Small hardware measurements informed simulations of spoken-digit recognition and early seizure detection. The larger application tasks were simulated rather than demonstrated as a deployed medical device. [4](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/brain-inspired-device-could-lead-to-faster-more-energy-efficient-ai-hardware)

### Progress to track: Multimodal correspondence and computing efficiency

For the implant, the useful question is which optical activity can be predicted from surface signals. For the material platform, it is energy and task performance under a stated implementation; these measures cannot be added to a neural-electrode count. [2](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/transparent-brain-implant-can-read-deep-neural-activity-from-the-surface) [4](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/brain-inspired-device-could-lead-to-faster-more-energy-efficient-ai-hardware)

### Study context

The lab’s publication list connects device physics, brain measurements and computing. The 2026 university report explicitly identifies scaling and integration with conventional electronics as future work. [3](https://neuroelectronics.ucsd.edu/publications-2/) [4](https://today.ucsd.edu/story/brain-inspired-device-could-lead-to-faster-more-energy-efficient-ai-hardware)

## Chang Laboratory

**University of California, San Francisco · United States**

Investigators: Edward Chang

Areas: Neural decoding; Neural interfaces; Computational neuroscience; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Edward Chang’s UCSF laboratory investigates how the human brain perceives and produces speech and uses that knowledge to develop communication neuroprostheses. Its clinical setting also links functional mapping to the preservation of language during surgery. [1](https://changlab.ucsf.edu/) [4](https://changlab.ucsf.edu/publications)

High-density recordings sample cortical population activity during speech tasks. Computational models map these signals to text, sound or facial movements; the experimental distinction between an offline reconstruction and an online feedback system is important when assessing usability. [1](https://changlab.ucsf.edu/) [4](https://changlab.ucsf.edu/publications)

**Methods:** High-density electrocorticography; Speech sensorimotor mapping; Neural text and audio decoding; Streaming recurrent transducer models; Psychophysics and electrical stimulation.

**Signals:** Cortical high-gamma activity; Low-frequency electrocorticography; Attempted-speech signals; Synthesized voice and facial animation.

### Projects

#### Streaming brain-to-voice synthesis, 2025

*Published online clinical demonstration*

A clinical study in one participant with severe paralysis and anarthria decoded high-density surface recordings into continuously streaming speech. The model generated output in 80-ms increments and personalized the voice to pre-injury recordings. The increment is an update interval, not the delay from a speech intention to audible output. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-025-01905-6)

#### Speech and an animated avatar, 2023

*Published single-participant milestone*

An earlier study used a 253-channel surface interface to decode text, voice and facial-avatar movements in one participant after brainstem stroke. Its text benchmark reached a median 78 words per minute with a 25% median word error rate on the reported task. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-023-06443-4)

### Progress to track: End-to-end communication and timing

Channel number is one system property. Streaming update interval, onset latency, intelligibility, error rate and training requirements address different practical bottlenecks and should be reported separately. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-023-06443-4) [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-025-01905-6)

### Study context

The laboratory provides a direct bridge from speech neuroscience to clinical interface experiments. Its 2025 publication list also includes individualized stimulation research; broad communication restoration remains an investigational programme. [1](https://changlab.ucsf.edu/) [4](https://changlab.ucsf.edu/publications)

Editorial question: Can a streaming system keep the user’s intended timing and voice while remaining reliable during spontaneous conversation?

## Bioelectronics Laboratory

**University of Cambridge · United Kingdom**

Investigators: George Malliaras

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neural interfaces

George Malliaras’s Cambridge Bioelectronics Laboratory develops soft electronic interfaces that exchange signals with biological tissue. Its neural work emphasizes conformability and organic electronic materials, with newer biohybrid projects combining engineered interfaces and biological components. [1](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/Research/Neuroengineering) [2](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/People/Leadership) [4](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/People/Postdocs)

The laboratory uses materials and device design to improve contact with tissue without relying on rigid, penetrating geometries for every application. Its programme includes organic conductors and transistors, surface recording arrays and preclinical biohybrid interfaces. [1](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/Research/Neuroengineering) [2](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/People/Leadership) [4](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/People/Postdocs)

**Methods:** Organic electronic materials; Conformal surface microelectrodes; Mixed ionic–electronic devices; Rodent validation of biohybrid implants; Electrophysiological recording.

**Signals:** Cortical field potentials; Surface-accessible action potentials; Ionic and electronic currents; Preclinical functional measurements.

### Projects

#### Biohybrid neuro-implants

*Current preclinical development*

Current research-associate descriptions identify a Parkinson’s-focused biohybrid implant project begun in 2025 and in-vivo validation in rodent models of neurodegeneration. These descriptions establish an active research direction, not a demonstrated human treatment. [4](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/People/Postdocs)

#### NeuroGrid surface recording, 2014

*Published animal and intraoperative study*

The earlier NeuroGrid collaboration used thin conformal organic-electrode arrays to record field and spiking activity from the cortical surface. Experiments included awake rats and intraoperative human recordings. This is a historical materials-and-recording landmark that predates the present Cambridge programme. [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4308485/)

### Progress to track: Mechanical conformity and useful signal access

A soft surface array is assessed by how well it conforms, what signals it can distinguish and how performance changes with time. Surface spiking evidence does not mean every contact continuously isolates a distinct neuron. [1](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/Research/Neuroengineering) [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4308485/)

### Study context

The group’s neuroengineering page frames higher density and long-term recording as development goals. Recent staff descriptions support rodent biohybrid work; the cited material does not establish routine clinical use of these newer devices. [1](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/Research/Neuroengineering) [4](https://bioelectronics.eng.cam.ac.uk/People/Postdocs)

## Neuroengineering Research Laboratory (NER Lab)

**University of Campinas · Brazil**

Investigators: Leonardo Abdala Elias; Carina Marconi Germer

Areas: Computational neuroscience; Neural decoding; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

UNICAMP’s Neuroengineering Research Laboratory studies the neural control of human movement. The current FEEC directory names Leonardo Abdala Elias and Carina Marconi Germer with the laboratory. Its work joins human electrophysiology, biomechanics and computational models, including questions relevant to ageing and neurological disease. [1](https://site.fee.unicamp.br/leoelias/ner-lab) [2](https://site.fee.unicamp.br/leoelias/pesquisa) [3](https://www.fee.unicamp.br/pesquisa/laboratorios-de-pesquisa/)

The group compares the activity of motor units with force, posture and cortical signals. Neuromusculoskeletal models allow a proposed cellular or spinal mechanism to be tested against measurable muscle output. A particularly useful teaching point is that an EMG channel measures a mixture, while a decomposed motor-unit train is an inferred component. [1](https://site.fee.unicamp.br/leoelias/ner-lab) [2](https://site.fee.unicamp.br/leoelias/pesquisa) [3](https://www.fee.unicamp.br/pesquisa/laboratorios-de-pesquisa/)

**Methods:** High-density and intramuscular EMG; Motor-unit decomposition; EEG–EMG coherence analysis; Force and posture measurement; Multiscale neuromusculoskeletal modelling.

**Signals:** Muscle electrical activity; Motor-unit discharge trains; Cortical EEG; Plantar-flexion force and ankle torque.

Read abstract and methods; eight healthy adults, intramuscular EMG, EEG and visual-feedback conditions.

### Projects

#### Visual feedback and corticomuscular coherence, 2025

*Published conference paper; healthy-adult experiment*

A conference paper measured soleus intramuscular EMG and motor-region EEG in eight healthy adults performing isometric plantar flexion. Alpha-band coherence differed with visual-feedback gain and with feedback removal. The study links a concrete interface choice—how force is shown on screen—to the measured neural–muscular relationship. [4](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-031-77588-8_161)

#### Models of motor-unit drive and muscle force

*Dated programme examples*

The laboratory’s detailed research page describes multiscale ALS-related neuromuscular modelling and studies of common inputs to ankle-muscle motor units. The funding references largely date to 2013–2017; these are documented research lines, without assuming every listed grant is still active. [2](https://site.fee.unicamp.br/leoelias/pesquisa)

### Progress to track: Motor-unit coordination and behavioural output

For the 2025 experiment the key scale is eight participants under several feedback conditions. Coherence, force steadiness and model agreement are more informative than counting implanted brain electrodes for this laboratory. [4](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-031-77588-8_161)

### Study context

The lab seeks methods useful for motor restoration and assessment, grounded in basic human neurophysiology. The cited feedback study tests a mechanism in healthy adults; it is not a trial showing improved outcomes in ALS or another patient population. [1](https://site.fee.unicamp.br/leoelias/ner-lab) [4](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-031-77588-8_161)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which changes in motor-unit coordination remain detectable during realistic movement and can guide an individualized rehabilitation intervention?

## Meintjes Neuroimaging Research and CUBIC

**University of Cape Town · South Africa**

Investigators: Ernesta Meintjes

Areas: Neuroimaging; Computational neuroscience

Ernesta Meintjes’s UCT research uses magnetic resonance imaging to study brain development and the effects of exposures and disease. The current institutional publication record includes children and young people affected by HIV, neonatal white-matter development and MRI acquisition methods. [1](https://eraonline.uct.ac.za/converis/portal/detail/Person/20024856?lang=en_GB)

The research combines structural or functional MRI with cognitive and clinical measures. Functional connectivity describes statistical relationships between regional signals; it is not a direct map of synapses. Study design and comparison groups therefore matter as much as the number of imaged regions. [1](https://eraonline.uct.ac.za/converis/portal/detail/Person/20024856?lang=en_GB)

**Methods:** Resting-state functional MRI; Brain-network analysis; Bayesian statistical comparisons; White-matter imaging; Neurocognitive assessment.

**Signals:** Blood-oxygenation-related MRI signals; Regional functional connectivity; Diffusion-related MRI measures; Cognitive-test outcomes.

### Projects

#### Auditory-network connectivity in children living with HIV, 2025

*Published observational neuroimaging study*

A paper examined resting-state connectivity in 11-year-old children with perinatally acquired HIV and uninfected peers. It identified differences within auditory networks and between auditory and other regions, and compared network measures with neurocognitive outcomes. These are group-level associations rather than proof of a causal mechanism. [2](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/systems-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnsys.2025.1508516/full)

#### Developmental and acquisition-method research

*Institutional publication record; methods and cohort studies*

UCT’s current research portal lists work on white-matter properties in neonates exposed to HIV but uninfected, a 2025 systematic review of imaging and cognition in young people living with HIV, and a 2026 cardiac diffusion-MRI methods paper. The list shows both developmental applications and broader MRI methodology. [1](https://eraonline.uct.ac.za/converis/portal/detail/Person/20024856?lang=en_GB)

### Progress to track: Cohort design, image quality and brain–behaviour relationships

The useful comparisons concern age, exposure and clinical groups, acquisition quality, and reproducibility of imaging–cognition relationships. MRI regions and voxels are not electrode channels or individually recorded neurons. [1](https://eraonline.uct.ac.za/converis/portal/detail/Person/20024856?lang=en_GB) [2](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/systems-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnsys.2025.1508516/full)

### Study context

The work supports understanding and assessment of neurodevelopment. The 2025 study does not establish a clinical diagnostic cutoff or a treatment. Current leadership titles from the inaccessible department and centre pages are not asserted here. [2](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/systems-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnsys.2025.1508516/full)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Do the connectivity differences predict later functional outcomes in independent cohorts after accounting for motion and clinical confounders?

## Brain-Computer Interfaces and Neural Engineering Laboratory

**University of Essex · United Kingdom**

Investigators: Caterina Cinel; Ian Daly

Areas: Neural decoding; Neural interfaces; Rehabilitation & prosthetics; Computational neuroscience

Essex’s Brain-Computer Interfaces and Neural Engineering laboratory is co-directed by Caterina Cinel and Ian Daly. It spans non-invasive interfaces, neural prostheses, rehabilitation, neural-signal analysis and computational neuroscience. The shared laboratory brings together several investigators rather than representing a single hardware platform. [1](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering) [2](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/projects) [3](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/interests-and-expertise)

The group combines EEG and other physiological measurements with signal processing, machine learning and experiments on perception, movement and decision-making. Some projects seek an interpretable physiological measure; others test an interface or a stimulation strategy. Their endpoints should be compared within those tasks. [1](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering) [2](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/projects) [3](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/interests-and-expertise)

**Methods:** EEG classification and event-related potentials; EEG, fNIRS and fMRI connectivity analysis; Myoelectric prosthesis control; Neuromuscular electrical stimulation; Muscle and nerve modelling.

**Signals:** Scalp EEG; Event-related potentials; EMG; Haemodynamic imaging signals; Behavioural decisions and motor performance.
