*Funded research programme*

The published project list describes an Essex–Vestre Viken collaboration led by Sebastian Halder with Bjørn Juel and Gernot Ernst. It aims to identify EEG biomarkers useful for objective pain assessment by combining signal analysis with clinical pain expertise. The listed aims are not yet a validated pain diagnostic. [2](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/projects)

#### Motor intention and neurofeedback, 2024–2026

*Ongoing projects listed by university*

Current project descriptions include Muhammad Tariq Sadiq’s cross-cultural motor-intention decoding collaboration and Ana Matran-Fernandez’s investigation of neurofeedback for perimenopausal symptoms. These widen the laboratory beyond spelling and cursor benchmarks. [2](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/projects)

#### Neural interfaces and rehabilitation

*Research-method portfolio*

The expertise page documents cuff-electrode and EMG approaches to prostheses, stimulation–BCI integration and models of muscles and nerves. These methods connect accessible signals to useful assistive or rehabilitative actions. [3](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/interests-and-expertise)

### Progress to track: Transfer across people, tasks and conditions

For this programme, better performance can mean a decoder that generalizes across users or an interpretable signal that tracks pain. None of the cited project pages supplies a common electrode-count series for the whole laboratory. [2](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/projects) [3](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/interests-and-expertise)

### Study context

The university records active health-technology projects and clinical collaborations. Biomarker reliability and treatment effectiveness are research objectives requiring their own evaluation, rather than consequences of a project being funded. [2](https://www.essex.ac.uk/departments/computer-science-and-electronic-engineering/research/brain-computer-interfaces-and-neural-engineering/projects)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which physiological features survive changes in user, recording session and real-world context well enough to guide care?

## Laboratory for Biomedical Microtechnology

**University of Freiburg · Germany**

Investigators: Thomas Stieglitz

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation

Thomas Stieglitz’s Freiburg Biomedical Microtechnology laboratory develops the physical components that make neural interfaces practical: flexible electrodes, durable insulation, packaging, connectors and implant-system integration. Its work treats mechanical and electrical reliability as central parts of neuroengineering. [1](https://imtek.uni-freiburg.de/professuren/bmt/forschung%20/electrodesandinterfaces?set_language=en) [2](https://www.imtek.uni-freiburg.de/professuren/bmt/forschung%20/systemintegration) [3](https://www.imtek.uni-freiburg.de/laboratories/laboratories) [4](https://news.vm.uni-freiburg.de/de/newsarchiv/die-wiederherstellung-der-wahrnehmung-bleibt-eine-der-zentralen-herausforderungen-der-neurotechnologie)

The electrodes-and-interfaces programme uses polymer substrates including polyimide and silicone, thin-film fabrication and material characterization. System integration adds cables, feedthroughs, electronics and protective packaging. Testing the complete assembly matters because a reliable electrode alone does not guarantee a reliable implant. [1](https://imtek.uni-freiburg.de/professuren/bmt/forschung%20/electrodesandinterfaces?set_language=en) [2](https://www.imtek.uni-freiburg.de/professuren/bmt/forschung%20/systemintegration) [3](https://www.imtek.uni-freiburg.de/laboratories/laboratories) [4](https://news.vm.uni-freiburg.de/de/newsarchiv/die-wiederherstellung-der-wahrnehmung-bleibt-eine-der-zentralen-herausforderungen-der-neurotechnologie)

**Methods:** Polymer thin-film microfabrication; Flexible electrode design; In-vitro and in-vivo interface evaluation; Focused-ion-beam and electron microscopy; Packaging and helium-leak testing.

**Signals:** Recorded neural electrical signals; Stimulation currents; Electrode-interface measurements; Package integrity and material-failure observations.

Read the university summary of the 2026 Lancet Neurology commentary; treated as a perspective rather than trial evidence.

### Projects

#### Electrodes and tissue interfaces

*Current engineering research programme*

The official research page describes soft electrode structures and characterization across material, manufacturing and biological-interface questions. It also identifies the connection between prototyping and regulatory requirements. [1](https://imtek.uni-freiburg.de/professuren/bmt/forschung%20/electrodesandinterfaces?set_language=en)

#### Packaging and modular implant systems

*Current system-integration programme*

The integration programme develops interconnects, conformal or hermetic protection and complete assemblies. It addresses ceramic and titanium packages, telemetry and the practical combination of electrode arrays with electronics. [2](https://www.imtek.uni-freiburg.de/professuren/bmt/forschung%20/systemintegration)

#### Restoring sensory feedback, 2026

*Published commentary described by the university*

A university news item describes a Lancet Neurology commentary co-authored by Stieglitz on tactile, proprioceptive and thermal feedback. It identifies long-term evidence, realistic performance measures and lifelong support as translation challenges. This is a perspective, not a new efficacy trial. [4](https://news.vm.uni-freiburg.de/de/newsarchiv/die-wiederherstellung-der-wahrnehmung-bleibt-eine-der-zentralen-herausforderungen-der-neurotechnologie)

### Progress to track: Lifetime and system reliability

The relevant measures include insulation integrity, stable electrode behaviour, connector reliability and package leakage over time. A contact count without those measurements does not describe a complete implant’s useful capacity. [1](https://imtek.uni-freiburg.de/professuren/bmt/forschung%20/electrodesandinterfaces?set_language=en) [2](https://www.imtek.uni-freiburg.de/professuren/bmt/forschung%20/systemintegration)

### Study context

The laboratory’s engineering workflow links fabrication with validation and system integration. The recent commentary reinforces that sensory benefit, surgical burden and sustainable clinical support need to be evaluated together. [1](https://imtek.uni-freiburg.de/professuren/bmt/forschung%20/electrodesandinterfaces?set_language=en) [4](https://news.vm.uni-freiburg.de/de/newsarchiv/die-wiederherstellung-der-wahrnehmung-bleibt-eine-der-zentralen-herausforderungen-der-neurotechnologie)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which failure mechanisms limit useful lifetime once electrodes, electronics, packaging and repeated stimulation are tested as one system?

## NeuroBionics Laboratory

**University of Melbourne · Australia**

Investigators: Sam John

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation; Bioelectronics; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Sam John leads Melbourne’s NeuroBionics Laboratory according to the university’s 2025 research seminar page. The documented programme develops devices that interact with the nervous system, including minimally invasive interfaces and endovascular stent electrodes, alongside non-invasive physiological measurement. [1](https://clarkinstitute.unimelb.edu.au/news/gci-seminar-series-june-2025) [2](https://research.unimelb.edu.au/__data/assets/pdf_file/0008/5466464/successful-projects-MIPA.pdf)

Its engineering approach combines electronics and neuroscience with clinical and industry collaborators. Two recent university descriptions make the scope concrete: an optical route to estimating pressure inside the skull, and an endovascular neural-stimulation development project. [1](https://clarkinstitute.unimelb.edu.au/news/gci-seminar-series-june-2025) [2](https://research.unimelb.edu.au/__data/assets/pdf_file/0008/5466464/successful-projects-MIPA.pdf)

**Methods:** Endovascular neural-interface design; Biomedical electronics; Ocular imaging and pulsation analysis; Medical-device prototyping.

**Signals:** Eye-vessel pulsation; Estimated intracranial pressure; Electrically evoked motor responses as a development target.

### Projects

#### EyeCP non-invasive pressure assessment, June 2025

*Device-development project described in an official seminar*

A Melbourne seminar by Sam John and Bao Nguyen described a handheld concept that estimates intracranial pressure from imaging of blood-vessel pulsations in the eye. The source documents the proposed measurement principle and prototyping collaboration; it does not establish diagnostic accuracy or regulatory approval. [1](https://clarkinstitute.unimelb.edu.au/news/gci-seminar-series-june-2025)

#### Endovascular stimulation for hand movement, 2025

*Joint-PhD research project*

The university’s successful joint-PhD project list names John and IIT Kanpur’s Nikunj Bhagat for development of an endovascular neural stimulator aimed at hand movement after tetraplegia. The award records a research goal, not a demonstrated restoration outcome. [2](https://research.unimelb.edu.au/__data/assets/pdf_file/0008/5466464/successful-projects-MIPA.pdf)

### Progress to track: Access route and clinically useful measurement

For these projects, relevant progress would be a well-validated pressure estimate or selective stimulation through a vascular access route. The inspected sources do not establish a current maximum neural channel count. [1](https://clarkinstitute.unimelb.edu.au/news/gci-seminar-series-june-2025) [2](https://research.unimelb.edu.au/__data/assets/pdf_file/0008/5466464/successful-projects-MIPA.pdf)

### Study context

The sources establish John’s lab affiliation and two 2025 development directions. The main laboratory research and people pages remained inaccessible during this audit, so the previous broader project inventory and co-leadership claim are not repeated. [1](https://clarkinstitute.unimelb.edu.au/news/gci-seminar-series-june-2025)

Editorial question: How do the less invasive access routes compare with established reference measurements for accuracy, selectivity and long-term usability?

## Cortical Neural Prosthetics Laboratory

**University of Michigan · United States**

Investigators: Cynthia Chestek

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Rehabilitation & prosthetics; Bioelectronics

Cynthia Chestek’s Michigan research develops interfaces that translate neural or muscle activity into useful movement control. The programme includes intracortical decoders, peripheral nerve–muscle interfaces and strategies for activating a person’s own muscles. Current university records list appointments across biomedical engineering, electrical engineering, robotics and neurosurgery. [1](https://medschool.umich.edu/profile/912/cynthia-chestek)

The work treats decoding and the output device as a connected control problem. Regenerative peripheral nerve interfaces use reinnervated muscle grafts to expose motor-intention signals. Brain-to-body approaches pair cortical decoding concepts with functional electrical stimulation, while testing biomechanical coupling between joints. [1](https://medschool.umich.edu/profile/912/cynthia-chestek)

**Methods:** Intracortical population decoding; Regenerative peripheral nerve interfaces; Implanted muscle recording; Functional electrical stimulation; Closed-loop joint-position control.

**Signals:** Motor-cortex electrical activity; Electromyographic activity from reinnervated grafts; Wrist and finger kinematics; Stimulation-evoked muscle responses.

Read the abstract, author affiliations and methods summary; BMI and FES components were tested separately.

### Projects

#### Wrist and finger control components, August 2026

*Published non-human-primate experiments*

A study tested intramuscular stimulation in two monkeys and an intracortical virtual-hand BMI in one of them, including after temporary paralysis. It demonstrated simultaneous wrist/finger FES control and separate BMI control; the paper presents components that could be combined, rather than claiming that the full brain-to-muscle loop was demonstrated. [3](https://jneuroengrehab.biomedcentral.com/articles/10.1186/s12984-026-02091-7)

#### RPNI prosthetic-hand control, 2020

*Published human feasibility study*

The earlier study recorded large motor signals from regenerative peripheral nerve interfaces in people with upper-limb amputation and used them for prosthetic-hand control. It established a dated clinical proof of concept for accessing peripheral motor commands through reinnervated muscle. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8082695/)

### Progress to track: Independent control and stable useful signals

Degrees of freedom, success during functional tasks and stability across days capture this programme better than one brain-electrode count. The 2026 work explicitly tests the biomechanical interaction between wrist and finger flexion. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8082695/) [3](https://jneuroengrehab.biomedcentral.com/articles/10.1186/s12984-026-02091-7)

### Study context

The programme spans animal development and human prosthesis research. Restoring a virtual hand, electrically moving a limb and providing everyday independent function are different evidence stages. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8082695/) [3](https://jneuroengrehab.biomedcentral.com/articles/10.1186/s12984-026-02091-7)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can the complete decoder-to-stimulation loop preserve independent joint control during fatigue, changing posture and daily functional tasks?

## Willsey Lab for Brain-Computer Interfaces

**University of Michigan · United States**

Investigators: Matthew Willsey

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Matthew Willsey’s laboratory develops intracortical brain-computer interfaces for people with paralysis. The lab moved to the University of Michigan in August 2024. Its recent work combines dexterous digital control with clinical investigation of fully implanted communication technology. [1](https://www.willseylab.org/) [3](https://www.willseylab.org/home/news) [4](https://paradromics.com/news/paradromics-achieves-real-time-speech-connexus-brain-computer-interface/)

The research connects neurosurgical access, real-time decoding and tasks that users value. Finger-based control provides multiple continuous command dimensions for interaction, while the Connexus programme investigates speech communication through a separate implant system and clinical study. [1](https://www.willseylab.org/) [3](https://www.willseylab.org/home/news) [4](https://paradromics.com/news/paradromics-achieves-real-time-speech-connexus-brain-computer-interface/)

**Methods:** Intracortical motor and speech recording; Continuous finger-state decoding; Real-time game interaction; Clinical implant studies; User-centred functional evaluation.

**Signals:** Motor-cortex population activity; Attempted finger movements; Speech-related neural signals; Decoded text and synthesized audio.

Company primary announcement read; clinical observation is explicitly attributed and not treated as a published cohort result.

### Projects

#### Finger decoding and virtual quadcopter control, 2025

*Published single-participant clinical study*

A Nature Medicine study in one participant with tetraplegia decoded three finger groups, including two-dimensional thumb control, yielding four degrees of freedom. The participant used these commands to navigate a virtual quadcopter. The experiment addressed recreation and social participation as well as target acquisition. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-024-03341-8)

#### Connexus early feasibility programme, 2026

*Early clinical study; company-reported communication milestone*

The lab reports opening Michigan’s Connect-One study in February and a first long-term Connexus implantation in June. On September 14, Paradromics reported real-time self-chosen communication by the first participant and identified Willsey as an investigator. That update is company primary reporting, not a full peer-reviewed performance analysis. [3](https://www.willseylab.org/home/news) [4](https://paradromics.com/news/paradromics-achieves-real-time-speech-connexus-brain-computer-interface/)

### Progress to track: Command dimensions and sustained communication

Four decoded movement dimensions describe controllable outputs, not four recorded neurons. The Connexus milestone adds a different endpoint—real-time communication—whose accuracy and long-term reliability require study-level reporting. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-024-03341-8) [4](https://paradromics.com/news/paradromics-achieves-real-time-speech-connexus-brain-computer-interface/)

### Study context

The lab’s news distinguishes its 2025 temporary implant experiment, its investigational Blackrock-array programme and the later Connexus study. Research permission and successful demonstrations should not be read as routine clinical availability. [3](https://www.willseylab.org/home/news)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which interaction tasks provide meaningful independence and remain reliable outside a supervised research session?

## Aplin Group: Electrical Neuromodulation

**University of New South Wales · Australia**

Investigators: Felix Aplin

Areas: Neuromodulation; Neural interfaces; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Felix Aplin’s UNSW group studies how electrical waveforms interact with nerves and how those interactions might be used for pain treatment and motor rehabilitation. The official research programme spans peripheral ionic direct current, spinal glial responses and the timing of electrically evoked activity. [1](https://www.unsw.edu.au/medicine-health/our-schools/biomedical-sciences/research-and-impact/research-themes/research-groups/aplin-group-electrical-neuromodulation) [3](https://www.unsw.edu.au/staff/felix-aplin)

The group combines neural engineering with experimental physiology. It measures what nerves and spinal circuits actually do under stimulation, then tests mechanisms and device designs using methods such as electrophysiology, imaging and molecular analysis. This makes waveform choice and tissue response central to the work. [1](https://www.unsw.edu.au/medicine-health/our-schools/biomedical-sciences/research-and-impact/research-themes/research-groups/aplin-group-electrical-neuromodulation) [3](https://www.unsw.edu.au/staff/felix-aplin)

**Methods:** Ionic direct-current stimulation; Peripheral nerve cuffs; Single-unit and multiunit electrophysiology; EMG and microneurography; Cell imaging and transcriptomics.

**Signals:** Peripheral nerve action potentials; Spinal multiunit activity; Electrically evoked muscle responses; Pain-related behavioural and cellular measurements.

### Projects

#### Peripheral direct current in rodent pain models, 2024

*Published rodent experiment*

The published study applied direct current at the sciatic nerve and measured spinal responses to natural hindpaw stimulation in neuropathic and inflammatory pain models. It reduced nociceptive activity, but the results should not be summarized as a proven human analgesic or guaranteed preservation of all non-pain sensation. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12358189/)

#### Stimulation fidelity and cuff safety, 2026

*2026 publications listed by university; results not extrapolated*

Aplin’s current UNSW publication list includes work on peripheral nerve entrainment failure, limits of compound-potential measures for stimulation dosing and the safety of a soft metal-free silicone cuff. These titles document a current emphasis on the gap between a delivered pulse and the neural response it produces. [3](https://www.unsw.edu.au/staff/felix-aplin)

### Progress to track: Recruitment, timing and tissue response

Stimulation channels do not specify how many axons respond or how faithfully they follow a waveform. Relevant endpoints include recruitment, failure to follow repeated pulses, nociceptive responses and effects on tissue. [1](https://www.unsw.edu.au/medicine-health/our-schools/biomedical-sciences/research-and-impact/research-themes/research-groups/aplin-group-electrical-neuromodulation) [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12358189/) [3](https://www.unsw.edu.au/staff/felix-aplin)

### Study context

The programme links fundamental stimulation studies to device development. The detailed 2024 pain evidence here is preclinical; the 2026 titles are used to establish research direction without inventing outcome statistics. [2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12358189/) [3](https://www.unsw.edu.au/staff/felix-aplin)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can stimulation selectively change the intended pathway over time without unwanted sensations, activation failure or tissue effects?

## Bioelectronic Circuits and Systems / Denison Group

**University of Oxford · United Kingdom**

Investigators: Tim Denison

Areas: Bioelectronics; Neuromodulation; Neural interfaces

Tim Denison’s Oxford group develops physiological closed-loop systems: devices that measure activity, decide when or how to intervene, and assess the response. It combines microelectronics, control engineering, clinical neuroscience and the practical economics of delivering a therapy. [1](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/person/tim-denison/) [2](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/research/neurotechnology/research-areas/epione/) [3](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/research/neurotechnology/)

The group designs research instruments that can support early human studies and expose the risks and costs of translation. Oxford describes three connected themes: adaptive implants, less invasive stimulation and neurotechnology suitable for resource-limited settings. A clinician partner is involved in each project. [1](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/person/tim-denison/) [2](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/research/neurotechnology/research-areas/epione/) [3](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/research/neurotechnology/)

**Methods:** Low-power implant electronics; Physiological feedback control; Neural sensing and adaptive stimulation; Programmable magnetic and ultrasound stimulation; System and health-economic evaluation.

**Signals:** Physiological feedback signals; Neural electrical recordings; Stimulation responses; Clinical and functional outcomes.

### Projects

#### EPIONE chronic-pain programme, starting October 2025

*Funded research programme; stated development aims*

The six-year EPSRC programme joins engineering and clinical teams to develop technologies targeting pain-related circuits. Oxford lists implantable stimulation and drug delivery, transcranial ultrasound, neurofeedback and computational neuroengineering. Patients, carers and health-service partners contribute to the programme. [2](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/research/neurotechnology/research-areas/epione/)

#### Smart implants and therapy-discovery instruments

*Research instrumentation and early translation*

Denison’s group develops sensing and algorithmic components for adaptive bioelectronic systems. The official page describes research prototypes mainly at technology-demonstration readiness, including tools used in clinical neuroscience and to assess possible treatments. [1](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/person/tim-denison/)

### Progress to track: Control performance and useful clinical deployment

Relevant measures include whether a physiological signal can guide stimulation, whether the resulting intervention improves an outcome, and what deployment costs and burdens it introduces. Device channel count alone does not answer those questions. [1](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/person/tim-denison/) [3](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/research/neurotechnology/)

### Study context

Oxford identifies Denison as co-founder and chief engineer of Amber Therapeutics, while distinguishing university research from therapy development. EPIONE is a programme of proposed and ongoing studies; its launch is not evidence that the target conditions have already been effectively treated. [1](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/person/tim-denison/) [2](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/research/neurotechnology/research-areas/epione/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which feedback signal is stable and actionable enough to improve an outcome beyond a simpler fixed stimulation strategy?

## Litt Lab — Translational Neuroengineering

**University of Pennsylvania · United States**

Investigators: Brian Litt

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neuromodulation; Bioelectronics; Computational neuroscience

Brian Litt’s Penn programme joins epilepsy care, neural-interface engineering and computational analysis of large recording collections. The current Penn faculty page identifies him as director of the Center for Neuroengineering and Therapeutics and lists new 2026 work on seizure representations, stimulation and multimodal interfaces. [1](https://www.med.upenn.edu/apps/faculty/index.php/g324/p6213)

The research asks both how to record the brain and how to interpret those recordings well enough to guide an intervention. Flexible arrays address spatial sampling and wiring; network models and learned representations address heterogeneous seizure patterns across people. [1](https://www.med.upenn.edu/apps/faculty/index.php/g324/p6213)

**Methods:** Intracranial EEG analysis; Flexible active electrode arrays; Spatiotemporal representation learning; Seizure-network modelling; Neural stimulation and multimodal recording.

**Signals:** Seizure-onset intracranial EEG; Cortical field potentials; Spatial and temporal network features; Stimulation-evoked responses.

- [The seizure embedding map: a spatio-temporal transformer for comparing patients by ictal intracranial EEG features at scale](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13351835/) — Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Read the published abstract and introduction; explicitly preserves the failure to differentiate treatment or surgical outcome.

### Projects

#### Seizure embedding map, July 2026

*Published retrospective computational study*

A transformer-based study analysed 882 seizures from 102 people with drug-resistant epilepsy, accommodating different electrode layouts and implant types. Its representations grouped seizures by onset patterns and anatomical region. The clusters did not differentiate therapy or surgical outcome, so the result is a comparison framework rather than a proven treatment-selection tool. [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13351835/)

#### Flexible active surface arrays, 2011

*Published animal hardware demonstration*

A landmark collaboration integrated thin silicon transistors into a flexible electrode array to reduce the wiring burden of dense surface recordings. Experiments in cats mapped evoked activity, sleep spindles and seizures, illustrating how local amplification and multiplexing change the sampling problem. [2](https://www.nature.com/articles/nn.2973)

### Progress to track: Comparable information across patients

The 2026 denominator is 882 seizures in 102 people, with differing intracranial configurations. This is a dataset-scale advance, distinct from simultaneous electrode count on one implant. [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13351835/)

### Study context

Litt’s clinical and engineering roles connect device development with epilepsy treatment questions. The latest seizure-representation paper explicitly leaves prediction of treatment outcome unresolved; that limitation is important when considering decision-support use. [1](https://www.med.upenn.edu/apps/faculty/index.php/g324/p6213) [3](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13351835/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can seizure representations prospectively improve a treatment decision once imaging, symptoms and clinical history are included?

## Rehab Neural Engineering Labs — Sensorimotor Interfaces

**University of Pittsburgh · United States**

Investigators: Robert Gaunt; Jennifer Collinger

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Neuromodulation; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

Pittsburgh’s Rehab Neural Engineering Labs investigate interfaces that restore useful movement and sensation. Jennifer Collinger’s work connects population decoding with functional control, while Robert Gaunt studies sensory feedback and how stimulation recruits neural tissue. Their programmes extend beyond moving a cursor to the interaction between action and touch. [1](https://www.engineering.pitt.edu/people/faculty/jennifer-collinger/) [2](https://www.rehabmedicine.pitt.edu/people/robert-gaunt-phd)

Recording motor activity can supply a command for a prosthetic limb; stimulation of sensory pathways can provide feedback about its interaction with objects. The group evaluates the percept itself and the behaviour it supports, rather than assuming a detectable sensation is automatically useful. [1](https://www.engineering.pitt.edu/people/faculty/jennifer-collinger/) [2](https://www.rehabmedicine.pitt.edu/people/robert-gaunt-phd)

**Methods:** Intracortical population recording; Motor-intention decoding; Somatosensory cortical microstimulation; Psychophysical localization tasks; Functional prosthetic-control evaluation.

**Signals:** Motor and somatosensory neural activity; Evoked tactile percepts; Grasp timing and force; Task completion and object interaction.

Retained claims rebuilt from the listed pages. Recent publications or news were sought explicitly; dated examples are identified in the profile.

Read abstract and results context; three participants. Online publication is December 2024, although the issue date is June 2025.

### Projects

#### Stable and more localizable touch, published December 2024

*Published human study; journal issue June 2025*

A collaborative study in three people with cervical spinal cord injury mapped the locations and intensities of sensations produced by somatosensory cortical stimulation. Combining electrodes with overlapping projected fields produced stronger, more localizable sensations and supported more precise bionic-hand use. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-024-01299-z)

#### Motor decoding and long-term stimulation, 2026

*2026 journal papers and separately labelled preprints*

Collinger’s current publication list includes a journal paper on attempted grasp timing and force, a published long-term intracortical microstimulation study and preprints on attentional load and error damping. These are distinct studies and evidence stages, not one combined clinical outcome. [1](https://www.engineering.pitt.edu/people/faculty/jennifer-collinger/)

#### Peripheral, spinal and autonomic interfaces

*Documented research interests*

Gaunt’s faculty description includes recruitment by stimulation in cortex, spinal cord and dorsal root ganglia, plus restoration of bladder function as a research goal. This widens the programme beyond arm prostheses. [2](https://www.rehabmedicine.pitt.edu/people/robert-gaunt-phd)

### Progress to track: Useful sensory feedback over time

Percept location, intensity, repeatability and benefit during object interaction are central. Several electrodes may jointly create one percept; electrode count is not a one-to-one count of useful sensory experiences. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-024-01299-z)

### Study context

The programme includes human implant research alongside basic and preclinical work. The tactile study’s three participants establish a specific experimental result; they do not define performance for all injury patterns or all implant recipients. [2](https://www.rehabmedicine.pitt.edu/people/robert-gaunt-phd) [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-024-01299-z)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Can stable sensory feedback reduce errors and effort during daily tasks across users and years of implantation?

## Toronto Rehabilitation Engineering — REL and Motion & Adaptation Science

**University of Toronto · Canada**

Investigators: Kei Masani; Milos R. Popovic

Areas: Rehabilitation & prosthetics; Neuromodulation

Toronto rehabilitation engineering connects electrical stimulation, movement science and assistive technology. Current university sources document Milos Popovic’s broad neurorehabilitation programme and Kei Masani’s Motion & Adaptation Science Laboratory at Toronto Rehabilitation Institute. This entry follows those documented programmes without assuming the old REL website establishes their current organizational roles. [1](https://bme.utoronto.ca/faculty-research/core-faculty/milos-popovic/) [2](https://bme.utoronto.ca/faculty-research/core-faculty/kei-masani/) [4](https://bme.utoronto.ca/news/improving-mobility-after-spinal-cord-injury-kei-masani-lab/) [5](https://bme.utoronto.ca/news/functional-electrical-stimulation-sleeve-to-improve-rehabilitation-for-motor-control-impairments/)

Functional electrical stimulation activates muscles through peripheral nerves. The engineering questions include how to deliver that stimulation conveniently, how to combine it with meaningful practice and how to assess movement. Masani’s current programme adds neuromechanical models, real-time feedback and motion tracking for standing and walking. [1](https://bme.utoronto.ca/faculty-research/core-faculty/milos-popovic/) [2](https://bme.utoronto.ca/faculty-research/core-faculty/kei-masani/) [4](https://bme.utoronto.ca/news/improving-mobility-after-spinal-cord-injury-kei-masani-lab/) [5](https://bme.utoronto.ca/news/functional-electrical-stimulation-sleeve-to-improve-rehabilitation-for-motor-control-impairments/)

**Methods:** Functional electrical stimulation; Textile dry electrodes; Real-time visual feedback; Depth-camera motion tracking; Clinical functional assessments.

**Signals:** Muscle activation and force; Standing and walking kinematics; Hand-function and self-care measures; Stimulation comfort ratings.

### Projects

#### Mobility assessment and training, March 2026

*Current research programme; university demonstration*

A University of Toronto account describes Masani’s group combining stimulation with real-time visual feedback and depth cameras. The aim is to improve assessment and guide therapies for standing, walking and adapted exercise after neurological impairment. [4](https://bme.utoronto.ca/news/improving-mobility-after-spinal-cord-injury-kei-masani-lab/)

#### Wearable FES sleeve, 2024

*Device study reported by university*

A collaborative Popovic study incorporated carbon-based dry electrodes into a textile sleeve. The university report describes comparable muscle torque and lower reported discomfort than hydrogel electrodes in its tested conditions, while identifying durability and further clinical evaluation as remaining work. [5](https://bme.utoronto.ca/news/functional-electrical-stimulation-sleeve-to-improve-rehabilitation-for-motor-control-impairments/)

#### FES versus conventional therapy, 2022

*Published multicentre randomized trial*

A randomized trial enrolled 51 people with incomplete tetraplegia. Both groups improved self-care after therapy; there was no statistically significant between-group difference. Recruitment and protocol delivery were affected by the pandemic. [3](https://doi.org/10.3389/fresc.2022.995244)

### Progress to track: Functional improvement and practicality

The 2022 trial’s 51 participants and 40-session therapy programme measure clinical evaluation scale. Garment comfort, reliable stimulation and useful everyday movement address different parts of rehabilitation performance. [3](https://doi.org/10.3389/fresc.2022.995244) [5](https://bme.utoronto.ca/news/functional-electrical-stimulation-sleeve-to-improve-rehabilitation-for-motor-control-impairments/)

### Study context

Current institutional sources identify Masani with the Motion & Adaptation Science Laboratory. The older REL site could not be rechecked, so director/founder labels were removed. The controlled trial does not establish superiority of FES over conventional therapy. [2](https://bme.utoronto.ca/faculty-research/core-faculty/kei-masani/) [3](https://doi.org/10.3389/fresc.2022.995244)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which combination of stimulation, feedback and task practice produces benefits that persist and justify its added burden?

## Clinical Neurophysiology

**University of Twente · Netherlands**

Investigators: Michel van Putten

Areas: Neural decoding; Neuromodulation; Computational neuroscience

Twente’s Clinical Neurophysiology group connects engineering analysis with clinical neurology, especially epilepsy, brain ischaemia and intensive-care monitoring. Michel van Putten’s programme uses EEG and physiological modelling to ask what the measured signal says about brain state and how that information might guide care. [1](https://www.utwente.nl/en/tnw/cnph/research/) [2](https://www.utwente.nl/en/tnw/cnph/people-old/scientific_staff/vanputten/) [4](https://research.utwente.nl/en/organisations/clinical-neurophysiology/)

The group works closely with Medisch Spectrum Twente and uses patient recordings as well as computational models. Its research page includes EEG-based diagnostics, transcranial magnetic stimulation and neuromodulation for conditions including pain, tinnitus and epilepsy. Long-duration measurements allow within-person dynamics to be studied rather than relying on brief snapshots. [1](https://www.utwente.nl/en/tnw/cnph/research/) [2](https://www.utwente.nl/en/tnw/cnph/people-old/scientific_staff/vanputten/) [4](https://research.utwente.nl/en/organisations/clinical-neurophysiology/)

**Methods:** Long-term and subcutaneous EEG; Spectral analysis; Corticothalamic mean-field modelling; Clinical annotation of epileptiform events; Statistical patient-level comparisons.

**Signals:** Scalp and subcutaneous EEG; Epileptiform discharges; Aperiodic spectral slope; Model-derived network parameters.

Read the full abstract and bibliographic record in the institutional repository; did not inspect the article PDF.

### Projects

#### Candidate biomarkers from long-duration EEG, 2026

*Published observational biomarker study*

A study used two-channel subcutaneous EEG from ten people with pharmacoresistant epilepsy, assessing 14–16 days per participant. Model parameters and spectral slope were associated with epileptiform-discharge occurrence; the tested wave-area/spike-amplitude measure was not. Analysis eligibility differed by measure and event count. [3](https://research.utwente.nl/en/publications/candidate-eeg-derived-biomarkers-of-epileptiform-discharge-occurr/)

#### Clinical monitoring and mechanistic models

*Current clinical-neurophysiology programme*

The group’s broader programme examines epilepsy, ischaemia/stroke and intensive-care brain monitoring using EEG, computational models and stimulation methods. The aim is to improve diagnostics and guide treatments in the clinical setting. [1](https://www.utwente.nl/en/tnw/cnph/research/)

### Progress to track: Duration and within-person relationships

Two EEG channels can answer a valuable longitudinal question when recorded over weeks. The 2026 study’s ten-person cohort and 14–16-day analysis windows should not be converted into a large simultaneous neuron count. [3](https://research.utwente.nl/en/publications/candidate-eeg-derived-biomarkers-of-epileptiform-discharge-occurr/)

### Study context

The work is closely linked to patient care, but the 2026 measures are candidate biomarkers. Associations with discharge occurrence do not yet establish that adjusting treatment according to them improves outcomes. [1](https://www.utwente.nl/en/tnw/cnph/research/) [3](https://research.utwente.nl/en/publications/candidate-eeg-derived-biomarkers-of-epileptiform-discharge-occurr/)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Do the proposed markers remain informative during treatment changes and prospectively improve decisions for an individual patient?

## Restorative Technologies Laboratory

**University of Washington · United States**

Investigators: Chet Moritz

Areas: Rehabilitation & prosthetics; Neuromodulation; Neural interfaces

Chet Moritz’s University of Washington Restorative Technologies Laboratory develops interventions for movement impairment after spinal cord or brain injury. Its current faculty description includes hand-function studies after spinal cord injury, walking research in children with cerebral palsy and preclinical work on stimulation and plasticity. [1](https://rehab.washington.edu/faculty/chet-moritz-phd/) [2](https://www.ee.washington.edu/spotlight/moritz-spinal-stimulation/) [3](https://depts.washington.edu/moritlab/) [5](https://rehab.washington.edu/news-and-events/physical-therapy-division-research-highlights-from-spring-2026)

The programme combines stimulation with rehabilitation rather than treating electrical activation alone as recovery. Non-invasive spinal stimulation aims to change how spared circuits respond during practice; other projects investigate neural interfaces and biological repair mechanisms. [1](https://rehab.washington.edu/faculty/chet-moritz-phd/) [2](https://www.ee.washington.edu/spotlight/moritz-spinal-stimulation/) [3](https://depts.washington.edu/moritlab/) [5](https://rehab.washington.edu/news-and-events/physical-therapy-division-research-highlights-from-spring-2026)

**Methods:** Transcutaneous spinal cord stimulation; Task-specific rehabilitation; Hand and walking assessments; Brain-controlled stimulation research; Preclinical optogenetic and electrical stimulation.

**Signals:** Muscle activity and motor performance; Sensory and functional assessments; Autonomic physiological measures; Neural recordings in preclinical interface research.

### Projects

#### Hand-function improvement after spinal injury, 2024

*Published clinical study described by university*

UW reports a multicentre study in more than 60 people with tetraplegia, in which stimulation through skin electrodes was combined with rehabilitation. Seventy-two percent met the study’s combined strength-and-function improvement criterion after two months. The report also states that 28% did not meet that criterion. [2](https://www.ee.washington.edu/spotlight/moritz-spinal-stimulation/)

#### Autonomic function in subacute injury, 2026

*Published trial protocol*

A collaborative protocol proposes a pilot randomized inpatient phase followed by outpatient stimulation. It plans feasibility and safety measurements alongside exploratory cardiovascular and pelvic-organ outcomes. The protocol reports a study design, not evidence that autonomic function has already improved. [4](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0347211)

### Progress to track: Function, responders and sustained benefit

The meaningful scale includes participants, duration, task performance and the proportion reaching prespecified endpoints. A stimulation electrode count does not capture whether a person gains useful hand function or whether an autonomic intervention is feasible. [2](https://www.ee.washington.edu/spotlight/moritz-spinal-stimulation/) [4](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0347211)

### Study context

This programme spans clinical rehabilitation studies and animal mechanisms. Its current research targets include movement and autonomic function, but findings from chronic injury and planned studies in subacute injury should not be generalized across those populations. [1](https://rehab.washington.edu/faculty/chet-moritz-phd/) [4](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0347211)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which baseline features predict benefit, and how much of the improvement remains after stimulation and supervised training end?

## Neuromorphic Cognitive Systems

**University of Zurich · Switzerland**

Investigators: Giacomo Indiveri

Areas: Computational neuroscience; Bioelectronics; Neural decoding

Giacomo Indiveri’s Neuromorphic Cognitive Systems group at Zurich’s Institute of Neuroinformatics builds electronic systems that process information through events resembling neural spikes. Its research combines computational models with mixed analog/digital circuits and asynchronous communication between chips. [1](https://www.ini.uzh.ch/en/research/groups/ncs.html)

The group uses circuit physics to implement neural dynamics, rather than only simulating those dynamics on conventional processors. The goal is efficient real-time interaction with signals and the environment. This is brain-inspired computing; a hardware neuron is not a biological neuron recorded from an animal or person. [1](https://www.ini.uzh.ch/en/research/groups/ncs.html)

**Methods:** Mixed-signal integrated circuits; Event-based signal encoding; Spiking neural networks; Asynchronous inter-chip communication; Hardware–algorithm co-design.

**Signals:** Artificial spike events; Analog EEG used as input data; Device voltages and currents; Robot sensor and action streams.

Read abstract and implementation description; linear classification follows on-chip feature extraction.
- [A benchmarking framework for embodied neuromorphic agents](https://www.nature.com/articles/s42256-026-01197-w) — Abstract / publication record read; checked 2026-09-15. Read abstract, author contributions and code-availability section; subscription full text unavailable.

### Projects

#### EEG seizure detection on neuromorphic hardware, 2025

*Published hardware/dataset evaluation*

A study co-designed a spiking network and mixed-signal chip with an analog front end and event encoder. It processed signals from an EEG seizure dataset in real time; a linear classifier used the extracted features afterwards. The work supports an embedded-monitoring direction without establishing a fully deployed autonomous clinical monitor. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-025-99272-6)

#### DYNAP-SE2 neural processor, 2024

*Published processor characterization*

The processor supports configurable neural and synaptic dynamics including adaptation, short-term plasticity and transmission delays, with asynchronous routing and software configuration. It is a research platform for biological models and edge-computing tasks. [2](https://research.rug.nl/en/publications/dynap-se2-a-scalable-multi-core-dynamic-neuromorphic-asynchronous/)

#### Embodied neuromorphic benchmarking, 2026

*Published perspective and open-platform proposal*

A collaborative perspective proposes tasks, metrics and an open modular soft-robot platform to evaluate neuromorphic controllers in physical interactions. It emphasizes reproducible system-level comparisons rather than a single artificial-neuron count. [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s42256-026-01197-w)

### Progress to track: Task performance, energy and real-time operation

Processor neuron count is an implementation resource. Useful comparisons also specify signal encoding, external processing, energy, latency and the behaviour actually achieved. [2](https://research.rug.nl/en/publications/dynap-se2-a-scalable-multi-core-dynamic-neuromorphic-asynchronous/) [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-025-99272-6) [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s42256-026-01197-w)

### Study context

The programme connects neural computation with efficient electronics. The cited seizure study uses recorded data, and the 2026 paper proposes a benchmarking framework; neither is evidence that an autonomous cognitive or medical system has been clinically validated. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-025-99272-6) [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s42256-026-01197-w)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: When all sensing, encoding and output costs are included, which tasks retain a clear advantage from neuromorphic hardware?

## Biomedical Engineering Research Group

**University of the Witwatersrand · South Africa**

Investigators: David Rubin

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Rehabilitation & prosthetics

The Wits Biomedical Engineering Research Group combines neural interfaces with medical instrumentation, signal analysis and assistive technology. The current group page names David Rubin as group leader and lists expertise in brain-computer interfaces, rehabilitation, speech analytics, modelling and imaging. [1](https://www.wits.ac.za/eie/research-groups/biomedical-engineering/) [2](https://www.wits.ac.za/eie/research-groups/brainternet/) [3](https://www.wits.ac.za/future/stories/can-you-read-my-mind.html)

The programme emphasizes practical, often low-cost ways to measure the body and support interaction. Its neural examples use accessible EEG and eye-gaze signals; its broader computational work explores whether measurable speech or movement features can support clinical assessment. [1](https://www.wits.ac.za/eie/research-groups/biomedical-engineering/) [2](https://www.wits.ac.za/eie/research-groups/brainternet/) [3](https://www.wits.ac.za/future/stories/can-you-read-my-mind.html)

**Methods:** Non-invasive EEG acquisition; Eye-gaze and assistive-device control; Physiological signal processing; Speech acoustic and text features; Machine-learning classification.

**Signals:** Scalp EEG; Eye movements; Speech audio and transcripts; Movement and tremor measurements.

### Website review

Retained claims rebuilt from the listed pages. Recent publications or news were sought explicitly; dated examples are identified in the profile.

Status: reviewed. Checked: 2026-09-15.

Read the paper text from the official Wits repository PDF, including abstract, affiliations and methods; retrospective DAIC-WoZ study.

### Projects

#### Speech biomarkers for depression, 2025

*Published dataset study*

A paper by group member Vered Aharonson and colleagues examined acoustic and textual features from the DAIC-WoZ interview dataset. It used feature selection and random forests to classify depression labels. This is retrospective dataset research, not a validated replacement for clinical diagnosis or a direct recording of brain activity. [4](https://wiredspace.wits.ac.za/server/api/core/bitstreams/3c834887-783b-47ea-807f-4ffd37fee91a/content)

#### Brainternet, 2017

*Dated educational and interface demonstration*

The historical project streamed signals from a commercial EEG headset through a Raspberry Pi to an online interface. Wits describes it as one-way brain-to-internet transmission, supervised by Adam Pantanowitz. It demonstrated accessible data sharing, not unrestricted thought reading or bidirectional brain communication. [2](https://www.wits.ac.za/eie/research-groups/brainternet/) [3](https://www.wits.ac.za/future/stories/can-you-read-my-mind.html)

#### Assistive interaction

*Documented prototype portfolio*

The current group page describes eye-controlled interfaces and prototypes for people with disabilities, alongside brain-controlled robotic interaction. These illustrate the broader design problem of giving a user a reliable means to control their environment. [1](https://www.wits.ac.za/eie/research-groups/biomedical-engineering/)

### Progress to track: Accessibility and validated task performance

The relevant measures differ by project: useful device control, recording reliability or generalization of a classifier. Internet streaming does not increase the number of neural sources measured by the headset. [1](https://www.wits.ac.za/eie/research-groups/biomedical-engineering/) [2](https://www.wits.ac.za/eie/research-groups/brainternet/) [4](https://wiredspace.wits.ac.za/server/api/core/bitstreams/3c834887-783b-47ea-807f-4ffd37fee91a/content)

### Study context

The group connects engineering prototypes with clinical needs and frugal design. Prototype descriptions and benchmark classification results should be followed by evaluation with intended users and independent clinical data. [1](https://www.wits.ac.za/eie/research-groups/biomedical-engineering/) [4](https://wiredspace.wits.ac.za/server/api/core/bitstreams/3c834887-783b-47ea-807f-4ffd37fee91a/content)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which low-cost measurement and control methods remain accurate and usable for the people and settings they are intended to serve?

## Brain–Machine Intelligence and Qiushi BCI Research

**Zhejiang University · China**

Investigators: Gang Pan; Yueming Wang; Yu Qi

Areas: Neural interfaces; Neural decoding; Neuromodulation; Computational neuroscience

Zhejiang’s brain–machine research connects computer science, neural decoding and clinical collaborations. The inspected university pages and papers identify Gang Pan, Yueming Wang and Yu Qi across the State Key Laboratory of Brain–Machine Intelligence, the computer-science college and associated brain-interface research organisations. [1](https://person.zju.edu.cn/en/gpan) [2](https://person.zju.edu.cn/ymwang)

The programme studies how neural population activity represents actions and how a decoder can use that structure efficiently. Recent examples span attempted handwriting, low-power feature extraction and personalized emotion-state models. These are different tasks and evidence populations, not interchangeable measures of general mind reading. [1](https://person.zju.edu.cn/en/gpan) [2](https://person.zju.edu.cn/ymwang)

**Methods:** Intracortical array recording; State-dependent movement decoding; Computationally efficient signal features; FPGA and microcontroller implementation; Intracranial EEG and personalized models.

**Signals:** Motor-cortex spikes and spiking-band activity; Attempted handwriting trajectories; Gray- and white-matter intracranial EEG; Self-rated valence and arousal.

### Website review

Read abstract, affiliations, figure captions and publication date; full subscription article unavailable.
- [Legacy computer-science laboratory directory](https://www.cs.zju.edu.cn/_upload/article/files/d4/45/e46a2ca6469693738d84d1fffc3f/e3880619-c941-4906-a16a-a70e5a4ddc5e.pdf) — Page unavailable; checked 2026-09-15. The PDF could not be retrieved; no current programme claims retained from it.

### Projects

#### Chinese-character handwriting, 2025

*Published single-participant neural-decoding study*

A study used two 96-channel Utah arrays in one participant’s motor cortex during attempted handwriting. State-dependent models reconstructed stroke trajectories better than a fixed-tuning baseline. The reported 2,850 neurons were accumulated across 20 sessions; they were not simultaneous channels or a single-session neuron yield. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41562-025-02157-x)

#### Efficient intracortical features, April 2026

*Published algorithm and hardware evaluation*

The MAND study used differencing operations to extract spiking-band information and evaluated the method across human, primate and rodent datasets, with FPGA/microcontroller implementation. It addresses processing cost inside constrained systems; it does not itself demonstrate a complete chronic wireless implant. [4](https://www.nature.com/articles/s42003-026-10144-9)

#### Emotion-state decoding, July 2026

*Published human study; abstract inspected*

A study developed personalized models from intracranial EEG and self-rated valence/arousal in 18 individuals, then demonstrated real-time decoding in four new individuals. It examined cross-task generalization and contributions of gray- and white-matter signals. [5](https://www.nature.com/articles/s43588-026-01021-w)

### Progress to track: Task coverage, compute cost and denominator clarity

Physical recording channels, units observed across repeated sessions and people used to evaluate generalization are different denominators. The handwriting and emotion studies should be compared using their own tasks, populations and validation settings. [3](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41562-025-02157-x) [5](https://www.nature.com/articles/s43588-026-01021-w)

### Study context

These studies connect basic representations with potential clinical interfaces. Emotion decoding is based on experimental tasks and reported ratings; it does not establish unrestricted access to private thoughts or a validated treatment for affective disorders. [5](https://www.nature.com/articles/s43588-026-01021-w)

### Editorial notebook question

Editorial question: Which representations generalize across new tasks and sessions while keeping calibration and on-device processing costs manageable?

[Biomedical Engineering — Neural Engineering](https://bme.umich.edu/research/research-strengths/neural-engineering/)

Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Read the official research-area description, faculty roster and linked 2026 news through the web reader.

### University of Oxford · United Kingdom

Research-area directory links brain engineering, EPIONE, neural tissue engineering, drug delivery, neurofeedback and magnetic or ultrasound stimulation. Useful for comparing physical intervention methods with sensing and computational approaches.

[Institute of Biomedical Engineering: Neurotechnology](https://ibme.ox.ac.uk/research/neurotechnology/)

Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Read the research-area overview and linked group headings, including Brain Engineering, EPIONE, neural tissue engineering and non-invasive stimulation.

### University of Pennsylvania · United States

Official centre directory identifies the Center for Neuroengineering and Therapeutics and its connections to bioengineering, device development and clinical neuroscience. Follow the centre links for more recent project details.

[Neurology Translational Centers of Excellence](https://www.med.upenn.edu/neurology/tce.html)

Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Read the Center for Neuroengineering and Therapeutics section. Undated relative-age statements on the page are not used as current chronology.

### University of Pittsburgh · United States

Research-interest directory links neural engineering faculty and collaborators across stimulation, neural interfaces, optical methods, computational models and neural materials. Includes Gaunt and Collinger.

### University of Toronto · Canada

Faculty-linked research map covers neural engineering, rehabilitation, sensory systems and biomedical imaging. It connects seizure modelling and BCI work with functional electrical stimulation, movement science and medical imaging.

[Toronto ECE — biomedical research areas and researchers](https://www.ece.utoronto.ca/research/biomed/research/)

Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Read the research-area descriptions and researcher links for neural engineering, rehabilitation, sensory systems and imaging.

### University of Washington · United States

University-linked hub for neural interfaces, engineered plasticity, rehabilitation and neuroethics. Its dated research highlights connect engineering, physiology and clinical groups, including recent work on stimulation artifacts during assisted walking.

[Center for Neurotechnology — research and publications](https://centerforneurotech.uw.edu/)

Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Read the centre overview and dated 2026 research highlights, including the September 3 spinal-stimulation artifact paper. The historical NSF engineering research centre description is not treated as a current grant claim.

### University of Zurich · Switzerland

Institute research-group roster spans neuromorphic hardware, computational and experimental circuits, neurotechnologies and learning. Separate current groups from the previous-group section when following institutional histories.

[Institute of Neuroinformatics research groups](https://www.ini.uzh.ch/en/research/groups.html)

Page content read; checked 2026-09-15. Read the current and previous research-group sections; current roster includes Indiveri and other computational, experimental and neuromorphic groups.
